## The Deep Astral and Interstice

### 1. Summary

The astral is the space between worlds, the source of all magic, and the home of the beings who govern the cosmos. Clive explains it to Jason as a bowl of dumpling soup in which worlds float. The deep astral has no geography, time or space of its own, and gold-rankers rarely travel in it without a patron. Interstice, the universe-city, is the capital of the part of it that anyone knows.

### 2. How it works

- **The dumpling soup**: Clive says the cosmos is "a bowl of dumpling soup". Each physical reality is a dumpling, and "the astral is the soup through which we are all the dumplings... floating" (Book 1, Ch69 L97). The astral is also the source of all magic, "pure, unadulterated; the most fundamental building blocks of reality" (Book 1, Ch69 L99).
- **Why worlds differ**: some worlds soak up a lot of the soup and others very little, which is why Pallimustus has essences, awakening stones, quintessence and monsters and Earth did not (Book 1, Ch69 L99-101).
- **Astral spaces**: "think of the soup kind of congealing around a dumpling", giving a magical dimension attached to a world (Book 1, Ch69 L103). Congealed magic can drift away, especially if someone pokes a hole in the world's side, and can touch another world to form a brief unstable link (Book 1, Ch69 L105-107).
- **What the deep astral is**: "just magic. Only magic." Physical substance cannot exist there, which is why Jason's body "ceased to exist" when he was pulled through it (Book 1, Ch69 L113-115). Only his soul, being magical, survived (Book 1, Ch69 L125).
- **Interstice**: a physical reality barely the size of a small sun that has existed longer than most universes. It is a single flat plane with no sun or stars, containing only the city world of Interstice (Book 3, Ch69 L219). It is "both the oldest and largest metropolis in existence" (Book 3, Ch69 L220).
- **How the city looks**: oceans with interlinked artificial islands charged by great waves, hollowed mountains, volcano foundry cities and intelligent species of every stripe in jungles of ziggurats (Book 3, Ch69 L220). It has no sun yet has days and no moon yet has tides, and some call it "the capital city of the cosmos" (Book 3, Ch69 L221).
- **Who runs it**: "There were administrators in Interstice, but no rulers". When the great astral beings have business in a physical reality, this is the one they use (Book 3, Ch69 L222).
- **Arrival**: travellers arrive by dimensional teleportation in portal squares in the submarine bowels of the city, where local authorities note arrivals and make checks; portalling into just any region is frowned upon and magically obstructed (Book 3, Ch69 L224-225).

### 3. Later developments, Books 5 to 9

- **Dawn's description**: Interstice is "minute by the standards of a normal reality, but quite large by the standards of a city", more like a free-floating astral space, profoundly magical, and unique in that monsters do not manifest there. Many consider it the capital of the astral, "at least the portion of it that we know" (Book 5, Ch20 L67).
- **Borders and vessels**: the deep astral has no geography in any sense that works for physical reality. Astral spaces are borderlands where physical and astral blend, and the dimensional vessels powerful individuals use are mobile astral spaces (Book 6, Ch35 L39-40).
- **The wall**: Jason says that beyond the wall he sees in his spirit realm is the deep astral, shown as metaphor (Book 7, Ch1 L24). Every monster surge weakens the dimensional wall between a universe and the deep astral (Book 7, Ch36 L14). Until the bridge is complete, anything that tries to travel it is dumped into the deep astral (Book 7, Ch55 L16).
- **Regions**: a "functional metaphor" has regions where the influences of beings meet. The All-Devouring Eye's region, where the speaker and Gordon come from, abuts Legion's, where "life and annihilation are neighbours" (Book 8, Ch55 L92).
- **Who lives where**: Dawn says great astral beings are in charge of the cosmos and inhabit the deep astral, gods are like regional managers of universes, and astral kings are sentient miniature universes (Book 8, Ch67 L53).
- **Interstice and Jason**: once Jason reaches diamond rank, Shade can guide him to Interstice, and Haliastur will introduce him around (Book 9, Ch78 L25-27).
- **Power from it**: Jason's astral gate taps the deep astral, "the infinite plane of raw magic and dimensional forces" (Book 9, Ch95 L16, L33).

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **"Jason's body changed."** Knowledge's account is that his body was changed, but Clive insists it simply stopped existing (Book 1, Ch69 L115-117).
- **"Interstice has rulers."** It has administrators only (Book 3, Ch69 L222).
- **"The deep astral is somewhere you can travel to."** It has no geography; distance is a metaphor and only becomes real where physical and astral meet (Book 6, Ch35 L39).

### 6. Using this in a story

Use the astral as the setting's ocean and its bureaucracy. Worlds are islands, ships are bubbles, and the cost of travel is time and rank. Interstice is a place of power in which even the powerful serve, and a character from the feeder universes is a good way to show how high the ceiling is and how few reach it.

---

## Death

### 1. Summary

The god of Death on Pallimustus governs the passage of souls into the astral, and is distinct from the Reaper, the great astral being who is "very big on the finality of death". The church certifies deaths and returns, opposes Undeath, and is dryly protective of the order of things. The deity changes guise, and the books speak of both a goddess and a lord.

### 2. Facts

- **Domain**: the Reaper is "very big on the finality of death. Some consider it the true god of death, as all our god of death governs is the passage of the soul into the astral" (Book 2, Ch25 L67). Where there is no god of death to guide the soul into the astral, it drifts into the Reaper's grasp on its own (Book 3, Ch69 L189).

### Books 4 to 9

- **The god and the Reaper work in balance**: the Goddess of Death and the Reaper "each have their areas of authority and they work with an ebb and flow" (Book 7, Ch60 L100). The Reaper's cult may not trespass on the local god's domain (Book 4, Ch3 L30).
- **Precautions against dying**: Death keeps a close eye on resurrections. A priest tells Jason that the goddess knew "each time you had fallen and each time you had returned" the moment he entered her sphere, and has asked that he be more careful (Book 7, Ch9 L123, L125).
- **Certification**: the Church of Death confirms that returned adventurers are who they claim, and its paperwork helps with registration (Book 7, Ch7 L64; Book 7, Ch11 L83; Book 7, Ch17 L105).
- **Alignment with a cult**: the Church of Death is "closely aligned" with a cult that works with it, doing public-facing things while the cult is the hidden hand (Book 9, Ch27 L87).

### 3. Later developments (spoilers)

### 4. Using this in a story

Death is patient, fair and unimpressed. She is tired of people who will not stay dead and relieved when someone else deals with Undeath. A scene with her should feel like a meeting with a good-natured official whose job is also the end of everything. Her church is a bureaucracy of certificates, funerals and quiet help against the undead.

---

## Dominion

### 1. Summary

Dominion is the god of rulership, the god "of deciding who gets to be king". He is young, handsome, sneering, charming and frank, he is the god most interested in Jason, and he turns up in person more often than any other. His church is the outlier among churches in keeping low-ranking essence users under its thumb. For all his menace he gives Jason surprisingly honest advice.

### 2. Facts

- **The temple and the creed**: a believer in the Divine Square calls it the temple of Dominion and says "Dominion issues the divine right to kings and nobles. It is he who determines who rules, and who serves" (Book 1, Ch45 L15-18).
- **First appearance**: at Jory's clinic he comes to take the Healer's cast-out clergy, "young and handsome, but with a look of disdain and faint cruelty", with an aura like a wave of fire (Book 1, Ch95 L87). He tells those who join: "There is no service in my church... under me, you will obey or suffer. Or both, as I choose, because you will not enter my service. You will belong to me" (Book 1, Ch95 L94).
- **Jason and Gary**: "I love that guy," Dominion says of Jason, because "the ones who won't kneel are always the most fun" (Book 1, Ch95 L101). He tests Gary with his gaze, laughs when Gary stands, and says "That is where monarchs come from. I love them most of all. I'll be keeping an eye on you, Gareth Xandier" (Book 1, Ch95 L103-104). He asked after Jason by name (Book 1, Ch97 L7).
- **Thanks in person**: at the priest camp he comes with other gods in purple and gold robes with a simple golden crown, and tells Jason "You never disappoint" (Book 2, Ch65 L75-76). Jason calls him a "work acquaintance", someone he would not take for after-work drinks (Book 2, Ch66 L104-106).
- **His church**: the church of Dominion is the most prominent outlier among churches in keeping low-ranking essence users under its thumb (Book 3, Ch69 L859).

### 3. Later developments, Books 8 to 9

- **Oppression-friendly**: on Jason's lawn, Jason asks if he has somewhere to oppress, and he replies "I don't oppress. I'm just oppression-friendly" (Book 8, Ch56 L47-48). He compliments Jason's pagoda: "You're coming along nicely, Mr Asano" (Book 8, Ch56 L53-54).
- **Interest in Jason**: a priest says he can see why Dominion is so interested in him (Book 8, Ch43 L60), and Jason is warned that Dominion may have an agenda for him (Book 8, Ch59 L36).
- **Absent priests**: a character complains that she has never met a priest of Dominion, despite thinking power and control were the Church of Dominion's remit (Book 9, Ch49 L28-29).

### 5. Using this in a story

Dominion should be fun and dangerous in equal parts. He is charming, speaks his mind, never lies about what he wants, and enjoys defiance more than obedience. A scene with him should leave the character a little more informed and a little more worried. His church is the grim counterpart to his personality: strict, hierarchical and unforgiving.

---

## Fertility

### 1. Summary

### 2. Facts

### 3. Later developments, Books 6 to 9

- **Priestesses**: a priestess of Fertility is among those Humphrey's team meets (Book 6, Ch47 L910).
- **Food in a surge**: the Temple of Fertility keeps secure stations in outlying areas with infrastructure to grow large amounts of food quickly in a small space, critical to stopping fortress towns starving during a surge (Book 7, Ch21 L3-5). Jason leaves its main doors with his storage space loaded with supplies (Book 7, Ch21 L3).
- **Children without a partner**: the church can let people have a child without ever touching one another, growing the child in a jar, which is how most aristocrats do it (Book 7, Ch39 L74). A speaker says there are Gellers everywhere and that one would have to ask the Church of Fertility about that (Book 7, Ch45 L106).
- **Mixed-race births**: one person says the church allowed an elf and a human to have children and purged his father's "impurity" to produce him (Book 8, Ch17 L12), and describes another birth made possible with the church's help (Book 8, Ch20 L18).
- **Records**: a church record-keeper finds how the church helped Melody Jain have a child, which her order's overseers never knew (Book 9, Ch27 L26).

### 5. Using this in a story

Fertility is the unromantic god of life: logistics, food and births, with a surface of ancient frankness. His church solves problems that other churches cannot, so it is rich in leverage. Use its people to supply, to rebuild and to quietly keep records, and keep its murals as a running joke that the characters take less seriously than visitors do.

---

## Knowledge

### 1. Summary

### 2. Facts

- **Appearance**: in the Divine Square a glorious light appears in front of a temple and the people fall to their knees (Book 1, Ch45 L31). In her temple she takes the form of a woman with colourful clothes and Mediterranean features, "beautiful, yet there was something detached and untouchable about her" (Book 1, Ch46 L16). She says "I am Knowledge. Everything that is, or ever was known in this world" (Book 1, Ch46 L22).
- **Omniscience, limited**: she knows what anyone in her world knows, including what Jason brought with him (Book 1, Ch46 L22). Jason says in a recording that deities are gender fluid, "heard from the goddess of knowledge herself" (Book 1, Ch77 L8), and jokes that she knows his Mario Kart shortcuts (Book 3, Ch57 L87).
- **The church**: acolytes of the church check Adventure Society assessments, "don't try to slip anything past them, because there isn't any point" (Book 1, Ch38 L68). Gabrielle Pellin introduces herself as an acolyte (Book 1, Ch101 L72). Danielle Geller will send her scholarly relatives to the temple's library, as "the goddess always welcomes seekers of truth" (Book 2, Ch21 L129).
- **Gifts to Jason**: a token the goddess claimed would send him home (Book 2, Ch63 L48) and books on dimensional transgression, which Clive annotates (Book 3, Ch3 L14).

### 3. Later developments, Books 4 to 9

- **Answers**: Knowledge gives answers people need rather than those they want. In the Vitesse temple she tells Sophie, "Now is not the time to pursue this goal" (Book 6, Ch47 L614-616).
- **Temples**: the Rimaros temple is an important resource for magical researchers and has a symbiotic relationship with the Magic Society (Book 7, Ch9 L4).
- **Jason's survival**: Jason hopes it will not be a big deal that people knew he was alive because "Knowledge told people I was alive" (Book 7, Ch4 L58).
- **Armies**: Knowledge's church has been mustering forces around the world (Book 9, Ch42 L69), with a small holy army near Yaresh (Book 9, Ch36 L21; Book 9, Ch42 L80) that waits to strike the Yaresh force from behind (Book 9, Ch104 L76).

### 5. Using this in a story

Knowledge is helpful in the way a very good librarian is helpful: she answers the question that was asked, and also the question behind it, and she never lies. Her motives are the issue. A scene with her should feel civil and slightly chilling, because she has seen how the scene will go. Her church is wealthy, careful and influential, and a priest of Knowledge is both a source of information and a possible agent.

---

## Pain and Discord

### 1. Summary

Pain and Discord belong to the "hidden" gods, the gods people pray to for what they want to avoid. The books mention them only in passing. This file records every thing the text says, so that a story using them knows how much is open.

### 2. Facts

- **The hidden gods**: Carlos says many gods fall into two camps: those whose temples are anywhere there are people, and "the gods whose priests dwell in the hidden places. Gods who you pray to not because of something you want, but something you want to avoid. Pain, Discord, Deceit" (Book 8, Ch48 L51). These are the "dark gods" who "will do very well" if the Purity deception becomes public, and Carlos does not like "the idea of my actions serving the God of Discord" (Book 8, Ch48 L47).
- **Pain, a god or a goddess**: a soldier thinks of Pain as a god, but Kerr answers, "Where I come from, they know that Pain is a woman", and refers to "the Goddess of Pain's firstborn daughter" (Book 7, Ch25 L113-115). Gods are gender-fluid in the books (Book 1, Ch77 L8).
- **A byword for fear**: Trenchant says that against an affliction skirmisher he would "run like the God of Pain was chasing me" (Book 7, Ch44 L23).
- **Discord**: it is called out as "in particular" the god the deception benefits (Book 8, Ch48 L53).

### 3. Not in the text

The text gives no scene with either god, no named priest, no temple and no description of their clergy or appearance. The wiki's list names them as evil or hidden gods (wiki).

### 4. Using this in a story

Both gods work best as names that characters fear to say. If a story needs a church of Pain or Discord, it should be hidden, small and rarely seen, matching Destruction and Undeath, with priests who live where the text puts them: out of sight.

---

## Purity and Disguise

### 1. Summary

### 2. Facts

- **The church**: Anisa Lasalle is a priestess of the God of Purity (Book 1, Ch20 L80), and a priest of Purity in clean robes attends the Adventure Society inquiry team (Book 2, Ch17 L31). The temple has church militants who guard its interests (Book 2, Ch37 L105).
- **Collusion with the Builder cult**: when the church's involvement with the cult comes to light, a number of its key members vanish on "previously scheduled sabbaticals", including the archbishop Nicolas Hedron (Book 3, Ch1 L106). The people from the church are detained under the authority of the ecumenical council (Book 3, Ch5 L67).
- **Hendren**: the archbishop calls out, "You think afflictions can take down a priest of Purity?" in the fight (Book 3, Ch59 L45).

### 3. Later developments, Books 4 to 9

- **A bargain with the Builder**: Dawn says "the god Purity has struck a bargain with the Builder": Purity lays the groundwork and the Builder helps him cleanse the world of what he has come to see as unclean, taking the abundant astral spaces and leaving Purity to rebuild civilisation in his image (Book 4, Ch68 L139). Clergy of the Order say their "so-called God of Purity handed our world to a being that would taint our very souls" (Book 7, Ch16 L53).
- **Purification ability**: converts of the Builder cult have a purifying ability that comes ultimately from the god, passed along a chain from the god to a purified clockwork king, a purified core, and the converted (Book 8, Ch10 L24).
- **The ecumenical council**: it was convened by the churches to judge the church's behaviour, and found it had been operating well outside its dictates for some time (Book 8, Ch48 L32).
- **Unanswered**: Emir says people everywhere are trying to learn exactly when and why the original Purity was sanctioned. The churches either haven't been told or aren't saying, and the diamond-rank community has been digging into it (Book 9, Ch51 L15-17).

### 5. Using this in a story

---

## The Healer

### 1. Summary

The god of medicine and healing, the Healer is kind, modest in manner and strict with those who exploit the sick. His church runs clinics, hospitals and research. In Book 1 he casts out the corrupt clergy of the Greenstone temple in person, and in later books his priests, among them Neil, Carlos and Arabelle, are central members of Jason's team.

### 2. Facts

- **Corruption in Greenstone**: Neil Davone makes a stand in the Greenstone Temple of the Healer, saying "This isn't about serving the Healer", and is told he is no longer welcome in the church (Book 1, Ch94 L3-7).
- **The god arrives**: the Healer appears at Jory's clinic, tells his clergy that merely not being shunned from his temples is "mercy enough", and refuses to restore their essences: "For that, I shall give you no salve. But you may turn to another" (Book 1, Ch95 L84-86). He says the power-seekers are of no use to him and hands them to Dominion, who takes those willing (Book 1, Ch95 L90-95).
- **A blessing**: he declares the clinic a sanctuary for healing, accepts that Jory's alchemy includes "togetherness jelly" ("I'm not going to begrudge people a little... togetherness"), and promises rituals of sanctification and a healer to staff it (Book 1, Ch95 L120-126).
- **Those who repent**: he answers a plea from a priest with "You blame others for your failings?", and gives a slight nod of assent to those who accept responsibility (Book 1, Ch95 L106-109).
- **The church in practice**: Neil reminds enemies he is "part of the church of the Healer" (Book 3, Ch21 L46), and Carlos introduces himself, "First and foremost... I am a priest of the Healer. Before anything else, I'm here to help you" (Book 3, Ch22 L73). The church opposes forms of punishment such as necromancy techniques (Book 3, Ch32 L101).

### 3. Later developments, Books 4 to 9

- **Attackers' banner**: some of those who attacked the Order of the Reaper's fortress wore Healer robes, which Jason finds at odds with the picture of the church as intolerant, calling it "revisionist history" (Book 2, Ch58 L77-78).
- **Specialists**: Carlos specialises in soul damage from star seeds (Book 8, Ch17 L45). The church takes over from Purity in handling them (Book 3, Ch21 L73).
- **Research**: Carlos and three assistants from the Church of the Healer live and work in Jason's astral space (Book 9, Ch28 L44), and Carlos, Arabelle and Neil advise on the design of the hospital in Jason's palace (Book 9, Ch71 L38). Hana Shavar is High Priestess of the Church of the Healer in Yaresh (Book 9, Ch74 L114).
- **Compensation**: Neil says the Healer gave healing powers that can no longer resurrect the ability to heal "on a body-matrix level" to compensate for what Death took away (Book 8, Ch39 L146).

### 5. Using this in a story

The Healer is the gentle god whose gentleness has teeth. He does not argue and does not punish more than needed, but he does not give a second chance to those who treat the sick as a business. His priests are skilled and usually modest, and a team with one has a better chance of surviving a fight and a reason to listen to its healer's conscience.

---

## The All-Devouring Eye

### 1. Summary

### 2. Facts

- **Among those interested**: it is one of the handful of great astral beings that take an interest in physical realities (Book 2, Ch21 L92).

### 3. Later developments, Books 7 to 9

- **Its realm**: "The realm of the All-Devouring Eye" is where Gordon comes from, and Colin comes from the border territory with Legion's realm (Book 4, Ch32 L36).
- **No blessings**: Legion and the All-Devouring Eye "didn't hand out blessings" (Book 7, Ch11 L75).
- **Region**: it is "where I, and your friend Gordon, come from, and it abuts the realm of Legion, the great astral being whom administers the cosmic rules that govern life itself. Your sanguine horror comes from this place, where life and annihilation are neighbours" (Book 8, Ch55 L92).

### 5. Using this in a story

The Eye is the great silence behind Gordon. Keep it unreadable: no speeches, no demands, only effects. Anything it has caused looks like a coincidence until someone realises the pattern, and the beings who know it best are least willing to talk about it.

---

## The Celestial Book

### 1. Summary

### 2. Facts

- **Veneration**: "I venerate the Celestial Book," Clive says, describing a blessing that triggered a racial gift evolution, changing the human special-attack affinity to a spell affinity, like the elves (Book 1, Ch85 L22). It is "fairly common for those of us heavily involved in magical theory" (Book 2, Ch21 L94).
- **Not a god of books**: Jason first asks if it is "the god of books, or something" (Book 1, Ch85 L26). It is named among the great astral beings interested in physical realities (Book 2, Ch21 L92).
- **Approachable**: its stones are described as "a lot more approachable than the Reaper" (Book 2, Ch56 L94).
- **A word on proportionality**: the World-Phoenix reminds the Builder, "The Celestial Book wanted me to remind you about proportionality... The ritualist is one of his", meaning Clive, and the book will not tolerate a gold-ranker sent to kill the boy out of spite (Book 3, Ch69 L278).

### 3. Later developments, Books 9 to 11

- **A network**: those who venerate the Celestial Book "maintain a loose network, with ties to the Magic Society and the Church of Knowledge", and its symbol is commonly recognised (Book 9, Ch65 L28-30). A man on Jason's world venerates it (Book 9, Ch27 L21).

### 4. Using this in a story

The Celestial Book is the friendly face of the cosmos. It likes discoveries and cleverness, and it reacts to mortal pleasures with the delight of a student on a trip. A story that needs a patron for a researcher, or a great astral being who is on the player's side without being a tool, can use it. Its history of locking off essence combinations is a hook for anyone investigating why combinations are fixed.

---

## The Keeper of Moments

### 1. Summary

### 2. Facts

- **Domain**: "Even the Keeper of Moments, the great astral being that governs time, cannot [affect the past]" (Book 6, Ch14 L80).

### 3. Using this in a story

The Keeper is the quiet auditor of causality. Do not make it the one who rewrites the past. It prevents others from doing so. A story with time travel will bring in Raythe or her kind, and she will be polite, patient and immovable.

---

## Legion

### 1. Summary

### 2. Facts

- **No blessings**: Legion and the All-Devouring Eye are not known to hand out blessings (Book 7, Ch11 L75).
- **Domain and realm**: Legion "administers the cosmic rules that govern life itself", and its realm abuts the All-Devouring Eye's region, a place "where life and annihilation are neighbours" (Book 8, Ch55 L92). A sanguine horror was summoned from a "nightmare realm where the Limitless Legion met the Final Domain" (Book 9, Ch62 L23).

### 3. Using this in a story

Legion is a being of law: it cares about the rules of life, expects them to hold, and prefers a disciplined plan to improvisation. It is a good voice for a great astral being who is more worried about consistency than about people.

---

## The Builder

### 1. Summary

The Builder is the great astral being of construction, whose work is forging universes. There have been two. The first built the twin universes of Earth and Pallimustus and was sanctioned for it. The second, the Builder of the present, is a mortal messenger who was raised to the office and whose authority is held on conditions. He is obsessed with physical reality, runs the cults that hunt astral spaces, and is building a universe of his own.

### 2. Facts

- **Obsession and method**: "obsessed with physical reality while, by its very nature, being unable to co-exist with it", the Builder recruits people to carve off the astral spaces connected to their world, then steps in and claims them (Book 2, Ch21 L106-112). He gathers followers rather than crossing the World-Phoenix (Book 2, Ch25 L69).
- **Tools**: star seeds, tokens, cults, constructs and clockwork animals (Book 2, Ch21 L26-36; Book 2, Ch25 L37-45).
- **The three-way meeting**: the Builder meets the Reaper and the World-Phoenix, grandly says "I will be worshipped by an entire universe... A god beyond gods" (Book 3, Ch69 L261), and is told that "we are the rules" (Book 3, Ch69 L258). The Reaper's terms bar him from interfering with Jason's birth world or producing new star seeds, tokens or vessels (Book 3, Ch69 L288-292). In return the World-Phoenix will offer Jason a power at the cost of no further resurrections (Book 3, Ch69 L295).
- **Observers**: the Builder's observer constructs accompany a group at a test, and Shade reads this as the Builder testing them (Book 3, Ch63 L45).

### 3. Later developments, Books 6 to 9

- **The compact**: Dawn says Jason's killing by Shako violated the compact between the Builder, the Reaper and the World-Phoenix (Book 6, Ch18 L6).
- **The Book 8 deal**: the Builder, speaking at the table, says "Authority must be traded" (Book 8, Ch54 L19). Jason trades the authority token for the opening of the fundamental realm and the Builder's withdrawal from the planet, and the Builder replies "Accepted. Deal struck" before the World-Phoenix can object (Book 8, Ch54 L89-94). His voice is "like the grinding of stone" (Book 8, Ch54 L19).

### 5. Using this in a story

The Builder is a great power with a mortal's resentments. He is arrogant, impatient and bound by pacts, and what he cannot do directly he does through cults, constructs and vessels. His plans have an expiry date because the other beings may revert his mind, and a story can use that fear.

---

## The Reaper

### 1. Summary

### 2. Facts

- **Domain**: "The Reaper is very big on the finality of death. Some consider it the true god of death, as all our god of death governs is the passage of the soul into the astral" (Book 2, Ch25 L67).
- **Items and orders**: the Way of the Reaper is a set of skill books (Book 1, Ch50 L40), and the Order of the Reaper's teachings are collected in its legacy (Book 2, Ch60 L15). The Order of the Reaper used ancient knowledge of how to anchor such realms, "provided by the Reaper itself" (Book 2, Ch44 L91). Reaper awakening stones give mostly aggressive utility and conjuration powers (Book 2, Ch67 L45).
- **Speaking through Velius**: when the Reaper takes over its vessel, the warm voice becomes "cold and bleak" (Book 3, Ch69 L251). It tells the Builder that Jason "carried the World-Phoenix's token. Those pacts are older than you and I will not violate them" (Book 3, Ch69 L254).
- **The rules**: "We are the rules. To deny them is to deny ourselves" (Book 3, Ch69 L258). It arranges the compromise between the Builder and the World-Phoenix and makes its own concession, because it fears being made a farce if Jason is resurrected over and over (Book 3, Ch69 L284-304).
- **Shade**: "Of all my children, Shade has ever followed his own path", the Reaper says, with a brief fatherly smile (Book 3, Ch69 L305-306).

### 3. Later developments, Books 5 to 9

- **A favour**: Dawn says the Reaper offered to bring Farrah back, because it wanted to stop the World-Phoenix sending Jason's soul back and forth across the astral with tokens (Book 5, Ch20 L41).
- **The cult**: the Cult of the Reaper requires authentic veneration. It was among the first to notice its former offshoot order moving again, but is hard to infiltrate (Book 9, Ch27 L57).

### 5. Using this in a story

The Reaper is a stern parent with a dry streak. It follows its own rules strictly, bends them rarely and thinks in eons. It has no interest in grand speeches. If a story needs a great astral being to say no, or to set limits, the Reaper is the one.

---

## The World-Phoenix

### 1. Summary

### 2. Facts

- **The mark on Jason**: his first status screens carry a symbol of a fire bird, and an ability labelled "Authority of the World Phoenix" (Book 1, Ch1 L86). Jason says the World-Phoenix gave him "a gift as his soul passed through the astral", and that he is an outworlder through "happenstance" (Book 3, Ch69 L266-267).
- **Its role**: "the integrity of dimensions is mine. It is only with my permission that you can conduct your little game" (Book 3, Ch69 L272). The Builder will not cross it (Book 2, Ch25 L69).
- **The meeting of three**: the World-Phoenix attends in a celestine vessel with alabaster skin and ruby hair, "a teasing smile and an amused twinkle in her red, gemstone eyes" (Book 3, Ch69 L262). She calls Jason "a pleasantly effective little seedling" (Book 3, Ch69 L276), and negotiates with the Reaper over terms that forbid the Builder from interfering with Jason's world (Book 3, Ch69 L288-295).

### 3. Later developments, Books 4 to 9

- **How it acts**: "The World-Phoenix does not like to act directly. It prefers to set things in motion that will ultimately achieve the end it desires" (Book 4, Ch3 L66). Jason receives "the blessing of the World-Phoenix" (Book 4, Ch2 L39).
- **The cult**: its head is the First Servant, the ultimate arbiter before the World-Phoenix itself (Book 3, Ch69 L962). Dawn says she cannot sneak past the World-Phoenix (Book 5, Ch20 L52), and that it and she as its representative have always sought to sanction interference with the physical-astral boundary (Book 5, Ch66 L36).
- **Avoiding confrontation**: the World-Phoenix is "notoriously averse to direct confrontation" (Book 6, Ch35 L42). It crafts a bridge for Jason as compensation, Dawn says, "a sliver of hope in his darkest hour" (Book 6, Ch18 L65).
- **A tool that fails**: Dawn says the World-Phoenix "doesn't care about me or my wellbeing because it can't. What it can recognise is when a tool... is not functioning the way it should" (Book 8, Ch19 L54).
- **Speaking through Dawn**: at the table in Book 8, the World-Phoenix speaks through Dawn, "a lot more sultry than I expected", and thanks Jason for reminding Dawn of her mortality (Book 8, Ch54 L24-29). It grins and the vessel's eyes return to normal (Book 8, Ch54 L112).
- **Former servant**: Dawn is no longer First Sister, and her successor is Helsveth (Book 7, Ch63 L111; Book 9, Ch78 L28).

### 5. Using this in a story

The World-Phoenix is the patient gardener of the cosmos: it plants seeds, trims branches and tolerates a great deal so long as the dimensions hold. It speaks in a teasing, almost friendly way, and will use a friend against a friend without malice. A scene with it should leave the character feeling both looked after and managed.

---

## Abilities and Attributes

### 1. Summary

An essence ability is a power an essence user has from an essence. Each essence gives five; one is awakened at absorption and the rest are awakened later by stones. Each ability has a type, a cost, a cooldown and a rank, and gains a new effect at each rank. Four attributes, Power, Speed, Spirit and Recovery, each bond to one of the four essences and rise in rank as that essence's abilities are mastered. Abilities and attributes together decide when a person can move up a rank.

### 2. How it works

**The five abilities.** An essence "imbues 1 awakened essence ability and 4 unawakened essence abilities" (Book 1, Ch20 L52). The awakened one is a function of the essence and the person: Jason's dark essence gave Midnight Eyes at absorption (Book 1, Ch8 L35), blood gave Blood Harvest (Book 1, Ch21 L7), sin gave Punish (Book 1, Ch21 L50) and doom gave Inexorable Doom (Book 1, Ch21 L62). Further abilities come from awakening stones (see `Awakening_Stones.md`). A full set is twenty.

**Types.** The System sorts abilities into types that show on each ability's description, with keywords after them. The types visible in the books are special attack, spell, special ability, aura, conjuration and familiar (with summon and ritual tags), plus a separate list of racial abilities. Examples from Jason's screens:
- Special attack (melee, drain, wounding, blood): Leech Bite (Book 1, Ch27 L28).
- Spell (drain): Blood Harvest (Book 1, Ch21 L15).
- Special ability (dimension, teleport): Path of Shadows (Book 1, Ch22 L116).
- Aura (holy, unholy): Hegemony (Book 1, Ch40 L37).
- Conjuration (darkness, light, dimension): Cloak of Night (Book 1, Ch8 L62).
- Familiar (ritual, summon): Sanguine Horror (Book 1, Ch23 L62).

**Cost, cooldown, rank.** An ability screen lists a cost in mana, stamina or both, in words (low, moderate, and so on), a cooldown (none, or a time such as forty seconds) and a current rank with a percentage: "Current rank: Iron 0 (00%)" (Book 1, Ch22 L117-119). Mirage Step costs "low stamina and mana" with a forty-second cooldown (Book 2, Ch8 L174-175).

**Rank within an ability.** Within a rank an ability also has a level and a percentage, so the same spell reads "Iron 0 (00%)" when first awakened (Book 1, Ch34 L39) and "Iron 1 (19%)" a few chapters later with a stronger effect (Book 1, Ch39 L70-71). An ability keeps one effect per rank, so its description lists an iron effect, a bronze effect, and so on, and the later effects add to the earlier ones (Book 5, Ch25 L23-25 shows Cloak of Night). The full ladder for each of Jason's twenty abilities up to gold is in `Jason_Power_Set_Reference.md`.

**Ranking an ability.** An ability reaches its next rank as a separate event: "Ability [Midnight Eyes] (Dark) has reached Bronze 0 (00%)" (Book 3, Ch15 L47). Several abilities reach Bronze 0 in the same stretch of Book 3: [Sanguine Horror] (Book 3, Ch21 L121), [Hegemony] (Book 3, Ch21 L136), [Path of Shadows] (Book 3, Ch29 L23) and [Blade of Doom] (Book 3, Ch38 L5). An ability can be held back: "Ability [Cloak of Night] (Dark) cannot advance further until all attributes have reached silver rank" (Book 5, Ch25 L26).

**Attributes.** The four attributes are Power, Speed, Spirit and Recovery. Each essence bonds to one of them and raises it from normal to iron: "[Blood Essence] has bonded to your [Power] attribute" (Book 1, Ch21 L6), dark to Speed (Book 1, Ch8 L34), sin to Recovery (Book 1, Ch21 L49), and doom to Spirit (Book 1, Ch21 L61). "Master all blood essence abilities to increase your [Power] attribute" (Book 1, Ch21 L6). Once an essence's abilities are all awakened, the linked attribute "will advance in conjunction with lowest-rank" ability of that essence (Book 2, Ch8 L171).

**Rank progress.** Progress to the next rank counts essences complete: "Progress to bronze rank: 50% (4/4 essences complete)" (Book 3, Ch14 L98) and a smaller fraction for fewer completed essences (Book 1, Ch99 L65). All of a person's attributes must reach the new rank before an ability can go further (Book 5, Ch25 L26). See `Ranks_and_Advancement.md`.

**Knowing what an ability does.** Ordinary essence users must feel their way into a power by instinct; "unlike other essence users, Jason had never been forced to fathom out his abilities by feel", because he has System messages (Book 3, Ch20 L17). The Magic Society keeps records of what abilities do at later ranks, and Clive urges Jason to look them up (Book 2, Ch9 L31-35).

### 3. Variations and exceptions

- **Passive powers.** Auras are passive and projected; Jason notes that an aura power common to the force confluence is able to move things physically, which none of his can do (Book 8, Ch60 L5).
- **Racial gifts.** Races have gifts that interact with abilities: humans' aptitude for special attacks over spells (Book 1, Ch87 L91; Book 4, Ch40 L72), Kylie's vision confluence letting her awaken a perception power from each essence (Book 4, Ch42 L44).
- **Variable subtypes.** A spell can have "variable subtypes, contingent on effect" (Book 2, Ch50 L57).
- **Abilities that outgrow their text.** Jason notes that the wording of Cloak of Night has changed from earlier iterations and wonders whether his own perception shaped it (Book 5, Ch25 L27).

### 4. Examples from the books

- **Sophie.** Mirage Step is the last of her five mystic abilities (Book 2, Ch8 L170-171), and she awakened confluence abilities before her essence abilities, which Clive calls "quite unusual" (Book 2, Ch8 L45).
- **Clive.** His Enact Ritual makes drawing and performing rituals more convenient (Book 2, Ch6 L43).

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **Strong abilities are better.** Farrah and Rufus both lay out a scale from individually strong abilities to abilities that "underwhelm in isolation, but used together become very dangerous", with affliction specialists at the second end (Book 1, Ch22 L88-93). "Simple doesn't mean bad" (Book 1, Ch22 L90).
- **Combat power matters most.** Gary: "just having a bunch of destructive combat powers makes you less useful to a team", and a few good utility powers help one get jobs (Book 1, Ch22 L111).
- **An ability is its description.** The description may not reveal all later effects, and costs for lower-rank effects are removed when abilities rank up (Book 5, Ch25 L27).

### 6. Changes over time

---

## The Adventuring Profession

### 1. Summary

Adventuring is the work of killing monsters and clearing hazards for pay. In practice an adventurer is a licensed contractor: they take posted notices and contracts from a jobs hall, work in a team, and are paid in spirit coins and loot. Most are not heroes in the story sense, and the profession is as much about judgement and bookkeeping as combat. This file describes how the work runs. The organisation is described in `05_Organisations/Adventure_Society.md`.

### 2. How it works

**What an adventurer does.** Rufus: "The bulk of our job as adventurers is hunting them down before they reach that stage", meaning the aggressive end of a monster's life (Book 1, Ch19 L36). Farrah and Rufus both reject the idea that adventuring is "just going places and killing everyone you find" (Book 1, Ch19 L37-38).

**Notices.** A monster notice, "whether from a notice board or the Adventure Society directly, has three pieces of information, so long as that information is known. The name of the monster, or a description. The number of monsters, and the approximate location" (Book 1, Ch57 L33).

**The jobs hall.** The jobs hall is "an annex of the main administration building", "a moderate-sized room divided into rows by standing bulletin boards" with a desk where a clerk records contracts (Book 1, Ch63 L54; Book 1, Ch64 L5). Contracts for areas near the city are in the hall; "those further afield were posted on each town or village's noticeboard", and every month the Society sends people to patrol those areas for a full month away, "not a popular task" (Book 1, Ch64 L3). A director can change how notices are posted: Greenstone's new director stopped keeping a separate board for incentivised contracts (Book 1, Ch63 L81).

**Pay and bonuses.** The Society pays iron spirit coins for an iron-rank monster contract, ten up to a hundred depending on number, travel and difficulty; bonuses can run "all the way up to an awakening stone, although such a reward was extremely rare" (Book 1, Ch64 L12). The Society "was known to give out awakening stones for exceptional service, although rarely" (Book 1, Ch64 L81). As a rule, "we don't give out awakening stones to people for killing monsters above their rank. It would just incentivise people getting themselves killed trying to jump ranks" (Book 1, Ch91 L34).

**Badges and stars.** A new member gets a badge, a circular iron medallion embossed with a sword and rod crossed over a shield, "one-star, iron rank" (Book 1, Ch62 L61, L66). The ranking system "has two parts": the first is the member's essence rank, and the second is stars, "not an assessment of your power, but your judgement" (Book 1, Ch62 L68-70). Everyone begins at one star and the maximum is three (Book 1, Ch62 L70). One-star contracts "are pure monster hunts, with no complicated elements to deal with" (Book 1, Ch62 L70). Two stars mean "at least some level of actual, unsupervised responsibility", investigating "potentially dangerous situations or unknown phenomena", and leading small expeditions of one-star members (Book 1, Ch62 L82). Three stars means "they trust you to handle yourself when things get political", and three-star members are "expected to anticipate and manage consequences at a higher level" (Book 1, Ch62 L85-86). "You can expect to drop a star rank each time you increase a tier in power" (Book 1, Ch62 L91). The badge confers prestige, not authority: "any actual authority comes from the contract you are carrying out" (Book 1, Ch62 L64). Society officials are outside the rating system (Book 1, Ch62 L72). The badge does not track the wearer if their aura resists tracking (Book 1, Ch62 L55-59).

**Teams.** "Rick's team had an archetypal distribution of roles, with a couple of resilient front-liners, some damage specialists and a healer. Not every team could find a good healer... team dynamics were as much about the balance of personalities as the balance of powers" (Book 1, Ch66 L60). When a team loses a front-liner it needs a new one (Book 2, Ch15 L38; Book 2, Ch29 L8). A group works "as a team, instead of as individuals" against a bronze monster, directed by a leader (Book 1, Ch77 L60).

**Specialist or generalist.** In Rimaros the culture prefers specialists: Jason, with stealth, utility, mobility and summoning powers beside his afflictions, "in Rimaros, you aren't" an affliction specialist, "you're a dabbler; a dilettante" (Book 7, Ch5 L25). A Rimaros assessor reads him as "an affliction-using generalist" while another corrects it (Book 7, Ch44 L7).

**Loot.** Teams split loot as a group (Book 2, Ch21 L14) and keep records for splitting later: Jason "took out a notebook and recorded all the loot for splitting up later" (Book 2, Ch48 L61). Choices can reflect need: the team lets Jason and Henrietta split an elemental's loot because each has expensive summoning rituals to pay for (Book 3, Ch14 L79). Rufus gives spare summoning materials to Jason when dividing loot (Book 2, Ch63 L111).

**Retirement and longevity.** Silver-rank arena professionals "keep going until silver-rank longevity is no longer enough, at which point they retire and make their way to gold for the extended life span" (Book 2, Ch73 L14). Gary retires after Book 3 to become a full-time weaponsmith (Book 3, Ch69 L895). Silver-rankers in low-magic Greenstone are "semi-retired at best" (Book 1, Ch61 L32).

### 3. Variations and exceptions

- **Guilds.** Guilds are private organisations of adventurers (Book 1, Ch38 L52). During the Book 7 surge, in a city like Livaros "about three in ten adventurers are in a guild. One in ten is in what's considered a high-end guild... Yet guilds are given sixty percent of the quotas to fill" (Book 7, Ch12 L23). The Society handles guilds' quotas and leaves them to manage on their own (Book 7, Ch38 L7).
- **Surge quotas.** During a surge every adventurer has an action quota; contracts over and above it earn contribution points, tallied on weekly and overall leaderboards with rewards (Book 7, Ch12 L15). Portal users earn more points because of the demand for transport (Book 7, Ch20 L23).
- **Rule of thumb.** Rufus's rule of thumb is to handle monsters one rank up only with a good match-up (Book 2, Ch47 L59).
- **Arena sport.** Iron-rank monster-hunt events judge teams on performance against identical monsters (Book 2, Ch73 L37).
- **Non-adventurers.** Many portal workers "aren't even adventurers and do storage space transport for a living" (Book 7, Ch20 L23).

### 4. Examples from the books

- **Rick Geller's team**, with front-liners, damage dealers and a healer (Book 1, Ch66 L60).
- **Jason's first contract** logged at the jobs hall desk (Book 1, Ch64 L5).
- **Team Work Saw**, whose defender Henry holds a gold-rank converted at the Book 8 Purity attack (Book 8, Ch12 L5).

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **The badge is authority.** It is not; the contract carries it (Book 1, Ch62 L64).
- **Higher stars mean more power.** Stars measure judgement, not power (Book 1, Ch62 L70).
- **Adventuring is killing for sport.** Rufus refuses to work with those who relish it (Book 1, Ch19 L38).

### 6. Changes over time

- **Level 1:** Jason's one-star iron badge is the starting point (Book 1, Ch62 L66); Greenstone's jobs hall changes with a new director (Book 1, Ch63 L77).
- **Level 2:** In the Book 7 surge, orders and quotas replace ordinary contracts, and guilds carry most of the load (Book 7, Ch12 L15, L23).

---

## Afflictions, Boons and Damage

### 1. Summary

An affliction is a condition that harms its target for a time; a boon is a condition that helps. Both are named in brackets in System messages, carry tags (poison, disease, curse, holy, unholy, magic and others) and can stack. Defence against afflictions is a game of resisting, cleansing and negating: a cleanse removes an affliction, resistance reduces the chance or strength of one landing, and some afflictions are built to defeat both. In this world a character who understands which tool beats which condition has a large tactical advantage, and Jason's power set is built around that interplay.

### 2. How it works

**Resistance.** Resistance is a chance and strength modifier. Jason's Sin Eater increases his own resistance to afflictions and gives him Resistant each time he resists or cleanses one (Book 1, Ch28 L93). His Hegemony aura raises allies' resistance and lowers enemies', and lowers it further for each instance of Sin (Book 1, Ch40 L41). In battle only a fraction land: "as many as one in three afflictions were taking hold" against a flesh monster in Book 2 (Book 2, Ch52 L51), and "almost half" against a lizard after resistance-lowering stacks (Book 3, Ch8 L76).

**Cleansing.** A cleanse removes afflictions. Jason's Feast of Absolution removes "curses, diseases, poisons and unholy afflictions", plus holy afflictions if the target is an ally, from a target other than himself, and it "circumvents all effects that prevent cleansing" (Book 1, Ch34 L40); a few chapters later, at Iron 1 (19%), the same spell removes holy and unholy afflictions from an ally or an enemy and "circumvents any resistance or immunity to cleanse effects" (Book 1, Ch39 L70-71). Cleansing triggers other abilities: "Cleansing afflictions has triggered [Sin Eater]" (Book 1, Ch34 L47). A cleansing aura, such as Cleansing Breeze, adds resistance to curses, diseases, magic afflictions, poisons and unholy afflictions and negates poisons in the air; it is a holy effect (Book 2, Ch9 L119).

**Cleanse conditions.** Many afflictions are written to resist cleansing: Mark of Sin "cannot be cleansed while target retains any instances of Sin or Legacy of Sin" (Book 1, Ch87 L99). A curse can protect another: a patient arrives with two curses, one adding poison to the other, and "the curse that kept making more of the other two couldn't be cleansed until the other curse was cleansed" (Book 1, Ch103 L130).

**Negating immunity.** Some afflictions remove an immunity: Weakness of the Flesh "negates immunities to disease and necrotic damage" and that "includes intrinsic immunities, such as from not having a biology or corporeal form" (Book 5, Ch31 L181). Blood From a Stone does the same for blood and poison, so "entities without blood can bleed" (Book 5, Ch31 L176), and Mortality does it for curses, even for the not alive (Book 5, Ch31 L179). Inevitable Demise, an aura Knowledge tried to give him, treated "any affliction immunities, including inherent immunities, as complete resistance" (Book 2, Ch13 L69).

**Holy and unholy.** Effects carry a holy or unholy tag, and some effects cannot touch them. Hendren's ongoing cleansing powers "could not eliminate holy effects", and he had only one power that could (Book 3, Ch59 L38). Another ability grants resistance to curses, magic and unholy afflictions, and bars unholy boons (Book 3, Ch67 L93). Hegemony is "both a holy and unholy ability at the same time", which fascinates Jason (Book 1, Ch40 L42).

### 3. Damage types

- **Physical and elemental.** Normal cutting, piercing, fire and similar damage. Rank gives damage reduction against lower-rank sources (Book 1, Ch21 L67).
- **Disruptive force and resonating force.** Two force types for special cases. Spell Breaker adds disruptive-force damage, "highly effective against magical defences and incorporeal entities" (Book 1, Ch56 L54); Stone Cutter adds resonating-force damage, "highly effective against physical defences" (Book 1, Ch56 L53). A defensive gift can reduce disruptive-force damage by a large amount (Book 2, Ch7 L162). An unarmed ability adds resonating-force damage and negates damage from actively intercepted attacks (Book 2, Ch8 L44). Gary's hammer for fighting constructs delivers "a resonating-force explosion" with each hit (Book 7, Ch31 L10).

### 4. Examples from the books

| Condition | Type | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| Bleeding | affliction, wounding | Ongoing bleed damage; absorbs healing; cannot be cleansed but removed after absorbing enough healing |
| Leech Toxin | affliction, poison | Reapplies Bleeding when negated |
| Creeping Death | affliction, disease | Ongoing necrotic damage; stacking |
| Rigor Mortis | affliction, unholy | Penalty to Speed and Recovery; adding to the stack inflicts necrotic damage |
| Weakness of the Flesh | affliction, magic | Negates immunities to disease and necrotic damage |
| Sin | affliction, curse | Necrotic damage taken increased; stacking |
| Mark of Sin | affliction, holy | Turns the target's aura into a beacon; cannot be cleansed while Sin or Legacy of Sin remain |
| Weight of Sin | affliction, holy | Transcendent damage if subjected to a holy boon, recovery or cleanse |
| Penance | affliction, holy | Ongoing transcendent damage; stacks drop off over time |
| Legacy of Sin | affliction, holy | Execute abilities have a greater effect on the target |
| Vulnerable | affliction, unholy | Resistances reduced; stacking |
| Resistant | boon, holy | Resistances increased; stacking |
| Integrity | boon | Ongoing life force and mana recovery; stacking |
| Blood Frenzy | boon, unholy | Bonus to Speed and Recovery |
| Harbinger of Doom | affliction, unholy | The target conjures butterflies that spread all its afflictions to other enemies |

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **Healing beats damage.** Bleeding soaks up healing and Leech Toxin reapplies it (Book 1, Ch29 L16).
- **Immunity is permanent.** Weakness of the Flesh, Blood From a Stone and Mortality cancel even intrinsic immunities (Book 5, Ch31 L176-181).
- **Rank is a wall.** Resistance and damage reduction from rank can be bypassed by transcendent damage (Book 1, Ch99 L169).

### 6. Changes over time

- **Level 1:** Early iron-rank afflictions are described plainly, like Vulnerable's "All resistances are reduced" (Book 1, Ch88 L132). Later versions gain extra clauses, such as the cleanse restriction and the intrinsic-immunity text (Book 5, Ch31 L176-181).

---

## Alchemy, Artifice and Magical Materials

### 1. Summary

Alongside essences and stones the world has a layer of working magic that does not need an essence: quintessence gems, spirit coins, potions, ointments, enchanted tools and growth items that rise in rank. This file describes what these materials are and how they are used. It does not list individual named items; those are in `09_Items_and_Artefacts/`.

### 2. How it works

**Quintessence.** Quintessence is "kind of like chunks of essence" and "gets used a lot more" than essences: "Ritual magic, alchemy, weapon forging" (Book 1, Ch19 L47-52). It appears as small gems named for their theme: dark quintessence is "small black gems" (Book 1, Ch7 L68). Water quintessence forms when magic-rich water from an astral space reaches the sea and condenses "huge quantities of magically-imbued water into a little crystal" (Book 1, Ch24 L40). Villages along the coast live by finding it (Book 1, Ch24 L48). Gary says he goes "through quintessence by the pile" for weapons and armour (Book 1, Ch19 L53). A quintessence gem can be used directly in an item: a bracelet "keeps the wearer cool and refreshed" with a water gem, and a gem refills its energy (Book 1, Ch31 L27-28; Book 1, Ch68 L114). A quintessence pile cannot be made into an essence (Book 1, Ch19 L48-50).

**Spirit coins.** A spirit coin is a crystal in the colour of a rank; the iron-rank coin "had the metal-grey colour of iron but was actually made of crystal" (Book 1, Ch22 L77). Eaten, it "dissolved with an intense fizzing sensation... tangy and energetic" and restores power (Book 1, Ch22 L77). Ranked people "sustain themselves on spirit coins and go without food entirely" (Book 3, Ch1 L5). A coin an hour or so can replenish reserves, with no food for a day or more (Book 3, Ch14 L18), and captors give an unconscious captive a coin to eat to keep him healthy (Book 3, Ch17 L26). Coins are grown on farms, where "special moulds cause magic to crystallise", and changes in ambient magic can ruin whole batches (Book 1, Ch80 L21, L43). A vehicle runs on coins fed into a slot (Book 1, Ch80 L104). The currency role is in `Economy_and_Money.md`; the exchange rates are "a full hundred lesser coins to one iron-rank coin, after which denominations went up in multiples of ten" (Book 1, Ch36 L72).

**Potions.** A recovery potion restores health, stamina and mana; "until the remnant magic fully dissipates, consuming further health, stamina or mana potions will result in toxic side-effects" (Book 1, Ch6 L53-54). Potions come in ranks: Jason's first is a bronze-rank, rare Recovery Potion (Book 1, Ch6 L46). Healers' patients are limited by cost: both ritual magic and alchemy have ways to heal, "but the cost and expertise required placed both out of the reach for most people" (Book 1, Ch35 L22).

**Alchemy.** Alchemy is brewing potions and elixirs. Jory's "true calling was alchemy", and his research is to make products cheaper and simpler: "If I can make alchemical products affordable to everyone, not only can it help a huge number of people, but it will open up huge new markets" (Book 1, Ch35 L10, L21). Alchemy and weapon forging use quintessence (Book 1, Ch19 L52). Alchemists belong to the Alchemy Association, which Jory joins alongside the Adventure Society (Book 1, Ch36 L28; Book 1, Ch38 L52). Alchemy sits beside medicine as an everyday tool: the shaving cream Jason mentions came from "an alchemist friend" (Book 1, Ch59 L8).

**Artifice.** Artifice makes magical items and devices. Farrah and Clive see "interdisciplinary crossover" between formations and artifice (Book 4, Ch25 L20). Clive mentions "some rather sophisticated artifice" used to tamper with carriages (Book 1, Ch82 L57). The Artifice Association sells good kitchen devices such as stand mixers (Book 1, Ch91 L58). Skilled people mix disciplines: a friend of Clive's has "solid ritual magic, a bit of artifice" and upgraded a mirage chamber (Book 2, Ch23 L72).

**Crafting.** Monster materials are crafting inputs: snakeskin and dark quintessence are crafting materials (Book 1, Ch7 L68). Gary makes weapons and armour (Book 1, Ch19 L53). Magic craftspeople range from leatherworkers and weapon-smiths to engravers and alchemists (Book 2, Ch42 L86).

**Growth items.** A growth item gains forms and powers as its rank rises when fed the right materials. Emir's cloud palace "is a growth item" (Book 2, Ch40 L61). Jason's cloud flask is "iron rank [growth], legendary", bound to him, and its upgrade to bronze needs 1000 bronze air quintessence, 1000 bronze water quintessence, 200 bronze dimension quintessence and 10,000 bronze spirit coins (Book 2, Ch74 L40-50). Legendary growth weapons come in matched sets (Book 2, Ch51 L98; Book 2, Ch53 L70).

**Soul-bound items.** A soul-bound growth item "cannot be used by anyone else" and "tends to take on" its owner's properties as it advances; Clive says that "if you meet someone who seems like a good person but has a hideous and twisted soul-bound item, stay clear" (Book 2, Ch74 L41, L72-74). Emir's cloud palace, "outrageously grandiose, yet welcoming and beautiful", reflects him (Book 2, Ch74 L74). Soul-bound items "are rare, even compared to other growth items", and "The advancement requirements are usually quite prohibitive" (Book 2, Ch74 L45).

**Communication.** Long-distance speech runs through the Magic Society's water link chambers: "Communicating through watery clones was the most accessible form of long-distance communication and a major use for the magical stone that Greenstone exported" (Book 6, Ch47 L843). The people on the other end appear as water clones "indistinguishable from her actual parents to the eye", although magical senses show them to be projections (Book 7, Ch40 L54). In a surge the Adventure Society takes over the links and cancels bookings (Book 6, Ch47 L922; Book 7, Ch28 L106). A carried device like "a water link chamber you carry around in your pocket" is mentioned as a communication network item (Book 7, Ch10 L38).

**Other tools.** Crystal wash is an odourless cleaner that "clears off everything"; its absence is a running problem (Book 1, Ch32 L57). Recording crystals capture images, messages and fights (Book 1, Ch54 L52; Book 1, Ch61 L129). Dimensional storage includes bags (Book 1, Ch17 L21); Jason's inventory has forty spaces, with stacking (Book 1, Ch61 L40).

### 3. Variations and exceptions

- **Low-magic zones.** Coin farms work only where magic is stable: "No activity, no life. Very stable ambient magic" (Book 1, Ch80 L21).
- **Divine supply.** A church can supply healing essences and stones (Book 1, Ch35 L23).
- **Prices.** Coins bought in bulk go through brokers; an adventurer can be paid in coins up to an awakening stone for a contract (Book 1, Ch64 L12).
- **Hydra whip and other loot.** Monster-part weapons keep some properties: a hydra whip "had the hydra's property of regeneration" (Book 5, Ch1 L49).

### 4. Examples from the books

- **Bracelet with water quintessence** (Book 1, Ch31 L27).
- **Skimmer powered by coins** (Book 1, Ch80 L104).
- **Cloud palace and cloud flask** (Book 2, Ch40 L61; Book 2, Ch74 L40-50).
- **Jory's clinic**, an alchemy lab and medical clinic in one building (Book 1, Ch35 L63).

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **Quintessence can replace essences.** It cannot (Book 1, Ch19 L48-50).
- **Healing potions are always safe to chain.** They cause toxic side effects until the remnant magic dissipates (Book 1, Ch6 L54).
- **Soul-bound items are interchangeable.** They are bound and reflect the owner (Book 2, Ch74 L41, L72).

### 6. Changes over time

- **Level 1:** Greenstone's market for essences and stones fell to an all-time low after the astral space was plundered (Book 3, Ch1 L100).
- **Level 2:** Cloud constructs advance with Jason's rank (see `09_Items_and_Artefacts/Cloud_Constructs_and_Palaces.md`).

### 7. Item types and where they are described

Each item type in `09_Items_and_Artefacts/` is explained here in one line and then in its own file. Rows from later books are under level tags.

| Item type | What it is | Full account |
|---|---|---|
| Guild badges | A Magic Society-made medallion, paired with a tracking stone, that carries a member's aura imprint and stars (Book 1, Ch62 L46-47) | `../09_Items_and_Artefacts/Badges_and_Guild_Membership.md` |
| Dimensional bags and storage | Bags have a rank and add slots to an inventory in proportion to it (Book 1, Ch76 L43, L49); portal abilities open only to places the user has visited (Book 1, Ch113 L58) | `../09_Items_and_Artefacts/Dimensional_Storage_and_Portals.md`; see also Unusual_and_Unique_Magic.md |
| Soul-bound growth items | Items bound to one soul that rise in rank when fed the right materials: Dread Salvation (Book 1, Ch56 L49-52), the Cloud Flask (Book 2, Ch74 L34-46) and the Robes of the Astral Verdict (Book 1, Ch7 L17-21) | `../09_Items_and_Artefacts/Soul_Bound_Items_and_Regalia.md` |
| Cloud constructs | Buildings and vehicles made of cloud: Emir's ship and palace (Book 1, Ch101 L64, L103) and Jason's flask forms (Book 2, Ch74 L34-46) | `../09_Items_and_Artefacts/Cloud_Constructs_and_Palaces.md` |
| Desert transport | Sand skimmers and larger sand barges that cross the desert (Book 1, Ch109 L21; Book 2, Ch40 L80) | `../09_Items_and_Artefacts/Desert_Transport_Skimmers_and_Barges.md` |
| Consumables and trial rewards | One-use items such as the Lesser Miracle Potion (Book 2, Ch44 L127-129), clockwork cores (Book 2, Ch25 L24-26) and star seeds (Book 2, Ch21 L26-28) | `../09_Items_and_Artefacts/Consumables_and_Trial_Rewards.md` |
| Awakening stones | Crystals that awaken essence abilities | Awakening_Stones.md and `../09_Items_and_Artefacts/Awakening_Stones.md` |
| Tokens | Transcendent-rank items from great astral beings and gods, including astral blessing tokens (Book 2, Ch63 L45-47) | `../09_Items_and_Artefacts/Tokens_Stones_and_Relics.md`; see Titles_and_Blessings.md |

| Item type (later books) | What it is | Full account |
|---|---|---|
| Regalia of the Dark Hegemon | Three soul-bound items joined into a set by the Soul-Imprinting Triune (Book 6, Ch4 L6-8) | `../09_Items_and_Artefacts/Soul_Bound_Items_and_Regalia.md` |
| Hegemon's Will and Dominion | The reforged sword and its scabbard, bound to Jason (Book 7, Ch54 L118-130) | `../09_Items_and_Artefacts/Hegemons_Will_and_Dominion.md`; see Jason_Power_Set_Reference.md |
| The Builder's door and the Fundament Gate | An astral construct that manifests as a portal, and the ring of standing stones Jason incorporates into his Spirit Vault (Book 5, Ch41 L24-26, L52-62) | `../09_Items_and_Artefacts/The_Builders_Door_and_Fundament_Gate.md`; see Astral_Spaces_Zones_and_Surges.md |
| Fundamental Realm Authority Token | A transcendent-rank token bound to Jason that opens gates to the fundamental realm (Book 8, Ch50 L87-91) | `../09_Items_and_Artefacts/Tokens_Stones_and_Relics.md` |
