## Astral Spaces, Transformation Zones and Surges

### 1. Summary

Three kinds of event reshape territory in the series. Astral spaces are pocket worlds attached to a physical world through apertures. Proto-astral spaces are short-lived, unstable versions of them that Earth suffers from. Transformation zones are sealed events that rewrite a place and the people in it. Monster surges are periodic increases in monster numbers across a world. All four are driven by the same source: raw magic leaking through from the astral.

### 2. How it works

**Astral spaces.** Farrah: "Astral spaces are pretty common, but normally they're sealed off and you need magic to track one down and break in. Being naturally open like this is rare, especially with so many apertures" (Book 1, Ch24 L38). Greenstone's river comes out of a large aperture (Book 1, Ch24 L38). An aperture connects a place on the physical world to the astral space; an astral space may have many apertures, and the mayor of one town notes theirs is "only one of many places with an aperture leading to that astral space" (Book 1, Ch31 L4). An astral space can be a rainforest island chain, with a sun that "just moving around on a circuit in the sky" and no night (Book 1, Ch104 L61). It can be far smaller than the world it is attached to: "the astral space is only a fraction of the size of the world it's attached to" (Book 1, Ch111 L24). Expeditions enter through the aperture, making camp, searching in teams and retreating through it for triage (Book 1, Ch104 L60-61; Book 1, Ch111 L28).

**Why they are attached.** Clive: "Astral spaces form attached to worlds, without which they immediately break down. Without a real world to anchor them, they cannot exist. But if an astral space is given the ability to sustain itself, even for just a brief period, the Builder can take it and anchor it to the world the Builder is creating from stolen parts" (Book 2, Ch21 L110). The Builder's process preserved the astral space at the cost of catastrophic destruction to the physical reality (Book 2, Ch21 L114).

**Surges.** "Every ten years there's a massive increase in the spawn rate of monsters. All across the world, all at the same time. Whole villages evacuate to fortified towns" (Book 1, Ch31 L71). The last one was eleven years earlier; "it's never exactly ten years. It's been as little as eight or as many as thirteen. The last few have all come pretty late" (Book 1, Ch31 L73-74). Most of the delta's population "comes into the city during monster surges" (Book 1, Ch35 L31). The Book 7 surge is "some five years overdue, during which time monster activity had gradually increased. Once the surge proper began, it was expected to be more brutal and last longer than any in history" (Book 7, Ch1 L56). During a surge adventurers earn contribution points instead of direct payment, and guilds use it to scout talent (Book 7, Ch38 L6).

**Proto-astral spaces (Earth).** A proto-astral space is "unstable dimensional pockets attached to the world" (Book 4, Ch37 L63). Their usual duration is "forty-three hours"; an anomalous one in Book 4 lasted "less than a second" and was the size and shape of a person, though its rated strength still registered above five (Book 4, Ch5 L127). The aperture to one "wasn't visible to the naked eye, although magical senses made it extremely easy to see. It was a more tenuous bridge across dimensional boundaries than a normal aperture and appeared... as if it might collapse at any moment" (Book 4, Ch40 L33). Entering one is announced by a System message: "The ambient magical saturation has increased. Your recovery rates will remain at normal levels without spirit coin consumption" (Book 4, Ch39 L55). Spirit coins cannot be formed in them: "Earth doesn't have the magic and coin formation takes months, so you can't do it in the proto-astral spaces" (Book 4, Ch37 L79).

**Zones as spaces to be claimed.** A transformation zone that Jason enters contains a genesis space, which his System names: "You have established a spirit domain. Expanding your spirit domain will define and stabilise unstable genesis space but trigger anomalous reactions from genesis space outside the spirit domain" (Book 6, Ch2 L15). Territories are claimed by defeating anomalies and then a final one: "You have overtaken a genesis space territory and purged all anomalous elements" (Book 6, Ch5 L32). See `Monsters.md` for the anomalies.

### 3. Variations and exceptions

- **Nitra.** A transformation zone at Nitra in Slovakia forms as a "glimmering dome several kilometres across" that stays closed for days (Book 5, Ch68 L68). The Chinese forces under Li Li Mei claim a site around it (Book 6, Ch5 L48).

### 4. Examples from the books

- **The desert's water-source astral space** in Book 1, an island-chain rainforest (Book 1, Ch104 L60-61); more in `06_Places/Astral_Spaces/`.
- **Kostroma** in Book 5, the first transformation zone, 43 hours long (Book 5, Ch44 L48).
- **Bankstown, Sydney**, a zone with lava streams (Book 5, Ch57 L11).
- **Nitra**, a Book 5 to 6 dome (Book 5, Ch68 L68).

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **Astral spaces are the same as other worlds.** They are small pockets attached to a world (Book 1, Ch111 L24).
- **Proto-astral spaces last forever.** They last about forty-three hours (Book 4, Ch5 L127).
- **A surge is exactly ten years.** It ranges from eight to thirteen (Book 1, Ch31 L74).

### 6. Changes over time

- **Level 2:** The first zones on Earth are 43 hours long and remake places in the pattern of other worlds (Book 5, Ch44 L48-52).

---

## Auras

### 1. Summary

An aura is the projection of a ranked person's soul. It grows with rank, is read by other ranked people as a sense of danger, strength and mood, and is how adventurers judge each other before words are spoken. Controlling the aura is a skill with three basic parts: projecting it, restraining it and suppressing other people's. Auras can be hidden, masked, aimed at normal people to intimidate them, and, at silver rank, begin to lift the body off the ground.

### 2. How it works

**Aura sense.** A new iron-ranker gains the ability to sense auras (Book 1, Ch21 L69). Jason's first impression of other ranked people is a feeling of danger, which Rufus explains is "our auras. Now that you're iron rank, you can sense them", and which gets more reliable as he learns to tell "a monster from an essence user from a regular person" (Book 1, Ch22 L31-33). The sense is "not completely reliable" because "some monsters can hide their auras. People can too, if they have an aura power" (Book 1, Ch22 L35). Ordinary people are "faint, barely detectable, while those with essences were much clearer"; a village normally has one or two with an essence, and a full-set iron-ranker "radiated out like a beacon" (Book 1, Ch35 L50).

**The three controls.** Farrah teaches Jason to control his aura "in three ways": "projecting your aura and restraining it", then "using your aura to suppress the auras of other people", which is "harder than the others", to be left alone "until you reach a certain level of proficiency" (Book 1, Ch40 L64-70). Projection is "pushing your aura out to affect people"; restraining is "drawing it in, whether to hide it, or just not be rude" (Book 1, Ch40 L66). Everyone projects until they learn not to: "It's what makes people with no aura control easy to read" (Book 1, Ch40 L74). Later the text repeats that "all aura control techniques were variations or extensions of those three" (Book 4, Ch74 L10).

**Why it matters.** Anyone with a perception power will eventually see auras clearly, so an uncontrolled aura reads like signs, including location and emotions, and "know when you lie" (Book 1, Ch40 L50). The skill with which an aura is controlled "is how adventurers make their first impressions on one another. If you can't do it properly, people won't take you seriously. Excellent aura manipulation marks you as an adventurer of training and distinction" (Book 1, Ch40 L80). Farrah calls the lack of an aura ability a serious gap: "anyone who hits bronze rank and can't manage their aura is a second-rate adventurer" (Book 1, Ch39 L152).

**A gold-ranker's aura.** At gold an uncontrolled aura is dangerous to normal people: "If you reach gold rank and can't control your aura properly, you can hurt normal people just by going near them" (Book 1, Ch40 L44). Such people are required to wear a suppressor: Farrah once saw "a gold ranker who forgot to put on an aura suppressor before going to a market. People were passed out, bleeding out their ears" (Book 1, Ch40 L46-48).

**Aura weapons.** Control can be turned on normal people. Jason, in Book 4, slowly unleashes his aura in a room: "Normal humans couldn't detect auras unless they were projected in a specific way. It was a simple use of basic projection control... a tool that essence users employed to intimidate normals" (Book 4, Ch7 L92). Higher-rank auras can be pointed at lower-rank ones: a bronze-rank woman suppresses Jason's with her own, which is "the very height of rudeness" (Book 1, Ch65 L91). A silver-ranker's weight can be felt without being a suppressing force (Book 1, Ch101 L16).

**Blending in.** A gold-ranker can use aura control to pass through a crowd unnoticed, and a high-end silver can do the same (Book 4, Ch74 L6). The technique blends projection and retraction to merge the aura into the ambient magic, with delicate suppression to blunt others' aura senses (Book 4, Ch74 L12-13). Normal people "were aura blind unless someone with aura control didn't want them to be" (Book 4, Ch74 L14). Jason's inspirations are Farrah's orthodox teaching, his own experience, Vermillion's use of aura on normals, the Mirror King's aura that "had felt like a part of the world around it", and Shade's masking (Book 4, Ch73 L95-100).

**Masks and suppressors.** Magic items hide or damp an aura. Farrah mentions aura suppressors (Book 1, Ch40 L46); a thief's methods include "aura masking" (Book 1, Ch100 L85); an aura mask is breached by using essence abilities or failing to keep a strong aura suppressed (Book 1, Ch102 L93); aura masking will not work against a tracking effect on the aura (Book 1, Ch103 L16). A suppression collar can negate a target's powers; Jason is able to "completely negate the effects of an iron-rank suppression collar" after practice, and holds off a bronze one for a few moments (Book 3, Ch24 L30).

**Silver and levitation.** "Levitation, Jason had discovered, was a perk of reaching silver-rank. It was an intrinsic property of a silver-rank soul, allowing the aura it projected to physically affect the environment" (Book 4, Ch73 L92). Farrah warns that it is "not even a practical ability. It requires intense concentration, has minimal effect, and is easy to disrupt with just some basic aura suppression" (Book 4, Ch73 L93). At silver it was "mostly just a power to levitate that helped their increasingly heavy bodies walk on weaker surfaces or even water" (Book 6, Ch14 L6).

**Aura signatures.** An aura has a signature that places can record: Emir's cloud palace stores Jason's signature in its registry (Book 2, Ch24 L13) and cannot let him in when it changes (Book 2, Ch66 L8). A soul crest carries a unique imprint on the aura that stays the same even when the aura changes, "impossible to track or falsify through even the strongest magic" (Book 2, Ch70 L37).

### 3. Variations and exceptions

- **Aura powers.** Some abilities are auras (Hegemony, Cleansing Breeze), which are a different thing from the aura itself: they are passive effects projected around the user (Book 1, Ch40 L37-41; Book 2, Ch9 L115-119). A person can have only one (see `Awakening_Stones.md`).
- **Physical force.** Most aura abilities cannot move objects. "An aura power common to the force confluence famously allowed auras to move things physically", and Jason's cannot (Book 8, Ch60 L5).
- **Perception and silver.** Farrah says her perception power does not improve her aura sense until silver rank (Book 1, Ch40 L52).
- **Monsters.** "Higher-rank monsters can" manipulate their auras; at low ranks the most to expect is "a few stealthy monsters that restrain their auras to hide better" (Book 1, Ch40 L76).
- **Transcendent echo.** Soul damage from gods can change an aura: after multiple divine presences press on him, Jason's "aura has been damaged by the direct, concerted focus of multiple transcendent-level entities", the recovery refines it, and it now carries "an echo of transcendent power" (Book 2, Ch65 L86-87).

### 4. Examples from the books

- **Titles from soul scars** (full list in Titles_and_Blessings.md). Jason's Interface uses titles to translate soul scars into game terms (Book 4, Ch2 L26). Resolute raises resistance to suppression from higher-ranked auras (Book 3, Ch14 L82); Indomitable enhances resistance and "ignores rank disparity", and adds a resistance bonus to allies in his aura (Book 4, Ch2 L23-24); Spirit Warrior increases both suppressive force and resistance, lets him resist other magical suppression, and lets him "attack their soul directly" after fully suppressing their aura (Book 3, Ch21 L151-152); Godless Prophet records the refinement after gods press on him (Book 2, Ch65 L85-86). Each changes his aura signature (Book 3, Ch14 L83; Book 3, Ch21 L153).
- **Rank disparity.** Aura strength is "the inherently superior strength of higher-rank auras", which some of Jason's titles do not cancel (Book 3, Ch21 L110).

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **Normals cannot feel them.** Normals can sense strong, directed auras on some level but are "aura blind" unless the wielder allows it (Book 4, Ch74 L14).
- **Suppressing an aura is simple.** Farrah calls it the hardest of the three controls (Book 1, Ch40 L70).

### 6. Changes over time

- **Level 1:** In Book 3 Jason refines his suppression until he can hold off an iron-rank collar entirely (Book 3, Ch24 L30).
- **Level 2:** After his Book 4 return to Earth, Jason rebuilds his aura control from scratch to use on normal people with "art and finesse" (Book 4, Ch72 L38).

---

## Awakening Stones

### 1. Summary

An awakening stone is a consumable crystal that awakens one essence ability the user has not yet awakened. It is the third kind of non-living magic Rufus lists, after essences and quintessence (Book 1, Ch19 L56). The stone does not choose the ability; it steers toward a theme, and what comes out depends mostly on the essences the user holds. Stones are how a new essence user fills out the twenty abilities that a full set carries, and they are bought, auctioned, given as rewards and kept as family wealth.

### 2. How it works

**What a stone does.** Each stone's description says the same thing: "Awakens an essence ability", with the requirement "Unawakened essence ability" (Book 1, Ch22 L99-102). Jason is shown a count of how many abilities he still has to awaken, such as "You have 10 unawakened essence abilities" (Book 1, Ch22 L132). Stones can also be used in other kinds of magic: "sometimes awakening stones get used as part of very powerful rituals" (Book 1, Ch22 L45), as the cult did with its feast stones in the sacrifice chamber (Book 1, Ch19 L57-58).

**What decides the ability.** Farrah: "the biggest determinate of what ability you get is the essence it comes from" (Book 1, Ch19 L67). A stone has a theme, and the theme sets the range of powers: a feast stone is "related to the concept of a feast, but that can manifest in any of hundreds of powers" (Book 1, Ch19 L63), which Farrah corrects to "thousands" (Book 1, Ch19 L64). The abilities a person has already, and the essences they have used, steer the stone. This is why Farrah and Rufus advise taking every essence before using stones: otherwise "your abilities will lack focus" (Book 1, Ch21 L36-37).

**Rarity and specificity.** "The more rare an awakening stone is, the more specific the powers" (Book 1, Ch19 L64). A common stone "can give you any ability the rarest could", it just draws from a larger pool; "rare stones don't give out better abilities, just more specific ones" (Book 1, Ch19 L66). The stones people seek are those with a desirable specialty, and "stones with desirable specialties tend to be the most expensive" (Book 1, Ch22 L104). A mid-rarity stone is "almost always a useful utility power" (Book 1, Ch22 L110). A high-rarity stone is "more specialised" (Book 1, Ch39 L147-148) and restricts the possible abilities to just one or two types (Book 2, Ch57 L36).

**Grading.** The Magic Society grades stones on a scale of one to five stars "based on how frequently they are known to appear world-wide", working with brokers and the Adventure Society; Jason's ability also sorts them into five stages of rarity: common (one star), uncommon (two stars), up to epic (four stars, "the second highest rarity") and legendary (Book 2, Ch9 L9, L17). In the Adventure Society's wording, a stone of the apocalypse is "unranked, legendary" (Book 1, Ch22 L128) and a stone of omens "unranked, epic" (Book 1, Ch39 L137).

**Using one.** The stone melts into the user's hand or arm and sinks in without fanfare (Book 1, Ch22 L71, L112, L152), and Jason's system confirms "You have awakened the blood essence ability [Leech Bite]" (Book 1, Ch22 L72). Most people need a ritual for each stone, and a person with a whole row "going through a half-dozen rituals would take hours" (Book 1, Ch22 L38-42). Clive's essence ability, Enact Ritual, makes drawing and performing the circles more convenient (Book 2, Ch6 L43). Clive draws the circle and completes the ritual when a team member uses an uncommon stone of preparation (Book 2, Ch66 L66).

**Hunger afterwards.** A stone of the feast leaves Jason ravenous: "I think the stone of the feast made it worse" (Book 1, Ch22 L75). The cure is a spirit coin (Book 1, Ch22 L76-77).

### 3. Variations and exceptions

- **Themes by name.** Stones are named for a theme or concept. The text names many: feast, adventure, apocalypse, omens, wrath, absolution, judgement, hand, foot, eyes, sky, purgation, preparation, rebirth, karma, avatar, celestials, moment, surge, champion, the stars, the Reaper, magus, fish, rain, ruin, focus, defiance and more. Jason's first stone is the "Awakening Stone of the Stars" (Book 1, Ch4 L40). A shopping list includes awakening stones of the eyes, the hand and the foot (Book 2, Ch5 L105-107). Neil and Jason select awakening stones of the avatar, and Humphrey selects an awakening stone of rebirth (Book 2, Ch63 L25). One speaker mentions an awakening stone of defiance (Book 9, Ch78 L129).
- **Tendencies of a theme.** The text gives these: eyes "a good shot at giving a perception power" (Book 2, Ch5 L105); hand awakens empty-hand abilities and attacks; foot, unarmed attacks and movement abilities (Book 2, Ch5 L108-110); sky, a very good chance of a flight power, though "it could just as easily give you a special attack effective against enemies in the air" (Book 2, Ch9 L29, L38); purgation, "almost certainly some kind of cleansing ability" (Book 2, Ch9 L47); magus, "a common awakening stone, especially with humans... almost guaranteed a spell, which humans rarely awakened, with an outside chance of a coveted summoning or familiar power" (Book 1, Ch87 L91); omens, a hope for an aura power (Book 1, Ch39 L150).
- **Human aptitude.** Humans have an aptitude for special attacks and rarely awaken spells without a magus stone (Book 1, Ch87 L91).
- **One aura only.** A person can have just one aura ability; the stone that would give a second does not work (Book 2, Ch13 L70). A tablet can remove an existing aura ability (Book 2, Ch13 L76-78).
- **Divine stones.** Gods craft stones for a specific outcome. Knowledge sends Jason two: a Divine Awakening Stone of Inevitability, to bestow an aura power (Book 2, Ch13 L60-62; the ability is [Inescapable Doom] in one line and [Inevitable Demise] in another, Book 2, Ch13 L62, L66), and a Divine Awakening Stone of Persistence, to bestow a specific spell (Book 2, Ch13 L83-85), with a Soul-Purgation Tablet to make room (Book 2, Ch13 L71-76). Jason shuts the case, calling it "a shiny red apple" and a poison pill (Book 2, Ch13 L96, L122). A god can take back what it gave: Jason says "the idea of some god repossessing my magic powers doesn't appeal" (Book 2, Ch56 L93).
- **Great astral beings' stones.** "The stones the astral beings produce aren't divine stones that the astral beings can revoke. They're just ordinary awakening stones whose aspect aligns with the great astral being in question" (Book 2, Ch56 L94). Relatively common from the Reaper and the Celestial Book, not from the World-Phoenix (Book 7, Ch11 L75).
- **Non-essence uses.** The same stone items fuel rituals (see `Rituals_Arrays_and_Formations.md`).

### 4. Examples from the books

Jason's own awakening record, with the stone where the text says which:

| Ability | Source | Seen at |
|---|---|---|
| Midnight Eyes | Dark essence, at absorption | Book 1, Ch8 L35 |
| Cloak of Night | Awakening stone of the stars | Book 1, Ch8 L51-57 |
| Blood Harvest | Blood essence, at absorption | Book 1, Ch21 L7 |
| Leech Bite, Feast of Blood, Feast of Absolution, Sin Eater | Stones of the feast | Book 1, Ch22 L72, L83-85 |
| Path of Shadows | Stone of adventure | Book 1, Ch22 L99, L114 |
| Sanguine Horror | Stone of the apocalypse (his arm turns blood red as it sinks in); the ability appears in the next chapter | Book 1, Ch22 L128, L152; Book 1, Ch23 L61 |
| Hegemony | Stone of omens | Book 1, Ch39 L137, L154 |
| Blade of Doom | Stone of absolution | Book 1, Ch88 L118-125 |
| Shadow of the Reaper, Hand of the Reaper | Stones of the Reaper | Book 2, Ch63 L73, L115 |
| Avatar of Doom | His final stone, awakened with eyes closed | Book 2, Ch64 L3-5 |

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **Rare means better.** Farrah and Rufus both deny it (Book 1, Ch19 L66; Book 1, Ch22 L104).
- **A stone can be aimed.** "There are no guarantees when it comes to awakening powers, not even with the rarest of stones" (Book 1, Ch22 L107). Clive: "I'm just managing expectations" (Book 2, Ch9 L40).
- **A stone saves you from needing a full set.** Rufus's advice is to take all four essences first (Book 1, Ch21 L36-37).
- **Stones are only for abilities.** They also fuel rituals (Book 1, Ch22 L45).

### 6. Later developments

- **Level 1 (Book 2):** Stones are rewards. The Adventure Society raised the bounty on Jason to a four-star stone for each person on the team that caught him, with a cap of six after people formed giant groups (Book 2, Ch9 L17-18). The Society gives out stones for exceptional service, rarely, and as rewards for contributions in open contracts (Book 1, Ch64 L81). A Geller family tradition supplies scions "with enough awakening stones to get started" (Book 1, Ch64 L80). As a rule, "we don't give out awakening stones to people for killing monsters above their rank", so that people are not tempted to jump ranks (Book 1, Ch91 L34).
- **Level 2 (Book 9):** Stones keep their reputation for surprising results. In a joking exchange, one speaker admits to using a stone of defiance and a companion teases that they expected an ability from a chef-type essence (Book 9, Ch78 L126-129).
- **Level 3:** After the throne is restored, stones continue to be used; the fixing of confluences, not stones, is what changes (see `Confluences.md`).

---

## Confluences

### 1. Summary

A confluence is the fourth essence a person receives. It is not picked, bought or found. It is produced by the three essences already absorbed, and it arrives the moment the third is used. In the main run of the series a given trio of essences always gives the same confluence, and the Magic Society keeps a list of the known trios. Confluences are rated and talked about as if they were essences in their own right, and many of the best-known power sets belong to a confluence.

### 2. How it works

**Arrival.** Farrah explains that "once you use your third essence, a fourth one manifests itself on the spot" and that confluences are "a result of the three essences you already have"; her own fire, earth and potent gave the volcano essence (Book 1, Ch19 L30). Confluences "only manifest after three essences are used", and cannot be found loose: "You can't find a volcano essence anywhere" (Book 1, Ch19 L31).

**The scene.** When Jason uses his third essence, three shimmering cubes rise from his torso and merge into one new cube; the System tells him the "confluence of your essences has produced the doom essence", which he "may claim or reject", and that a refused confluence "will not be available to you again" (Book 1, Ch21 L53-54). Rufus's practical advice is that refusing means one "can only replace it with a normal essence" and "I promise you that is not the way to go" (Book 1, Ch21 L57). Only the clergy refuse, because their gods give them divine versions of regular essences (Book 1, Ch21 L57).

**Looking them up.** The Magic Society's living document carries every essence and every combination it knows. A person can look an essence up to see what combinations are known and which are restricted (Book 1, Ch20 L97-104). Jason's dark, blood and sin is found there and produces doom, which is not restricted (Book 1, Ch20 L110). Clive can name a result from memory, as he does for swift, balance and wind (Book 2, Ch5 L83). The Society's common-combinations list is the basis for many choices, such as Ian's ministration confluence (Book 4, Ch71 L30). A larger list of "thousands of known essence combinations" is offered for sale to Jason by the Network's envoy (Book 4, Ch35 L123).

**Many routes, one result.** Some confluences are produced by many combinations. Mystic is common because "so many combinations produce it" and because "a lot of useful abilities come out of the mystic essence" (Book 2, Ch5 L87). Undeath comes from "many combinations", "almost all of which involve the death essence" (Book 1, Ch20 L19). Garuda has at least two routes, might, swift and bird (Book 4, Ch65 L22), and might and wing, which Jason secures instead of the plan to give Taika renewal and the immortal confluence (Book 4, Ch64 L57; Book 4, Ch65 L28).

**Confluence abilities come late.** It is "quite unusual to awaken the confluence essence abilities first", and some advocates of a theory say people with a very strong sense of self do it (Book 2, Ch8 L45-49).

**What a confluence does to a person.** A confluence can carry a racial or personal gift with it. Kylie's vision confluence lets her awaken a perception power from each essence, "where other essence users only had the one" (Book 4, Ch42 L44). An aura power common to the force confluence is able to move things physically with an aura, which is unusual (Book 8, Ch60 L5).

### 3. Variations and exceptions

- **Adventurer's confluences.** Farrah calls chimera "an adventurer's confluence", unsuited to a civilian such as Kaito, who is given a combination of common essences: vehicle, wind and swift, forming the soaring confluence, a known non-combat flight combination (Book 4, Ch71 L4-5, L36).
- **Surprising results from the user.** Greg's magitech confluence did not look like the Network's high-tech magitech users; Jason and Ketevan remark that he ended "all steampunk Tesla" (Book 5, Ch7 L64-66). The same confluence can play very differently.
- **Rare essences, common results.** Sword, myriad and adept give master as readily as sword, swift and adept, in a different style: Lance, a spell-using elf, shares the confluence with Valdis, who relies on special attacks (Book 3, Ch1 L89-90).
- **A deliberate choice.** Jason and Farrah pick sets by the kind of power wanted. Erika is given feast, knife and dance, producing bounty, a food-magic confluence (Book 4, Ch69 L102, L106), and Yumi takes blood, flesh and bone, which give the avatar confluence and let her remake her body into an idealised younger one (Book 5, Ch31 L147). Jason's two sets for the former Engineers of Ascension are sun, blood and life (avatar, "about as perfect an anti-vampire set as you'll find") or gun, hand and adept (master) (Book 6, Ch26 L94).

### 4. Examples from the books

The known pairings of essences and confluences, with sources, are in the table. Only cases where the book states both sides are listed; the full catalogue of essences is in `Essence_Catalogue.md`.

Further named confluences in the text include Medusa (Jason's joke about a hair essence plus snake and earth, Book 5, Ch3 L77), phoenix (Humphrey's sister, Book 6, Ch13 L69), and the lotus set Jason imagines for a Japanophile friend (Book 4, Ch72 L83).

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **You can pick your confluence.** Only the three essences are chosen; the fourth follows from them. People do pick a set to aim at a confluence, with the Society list as guide (Book 2, Ch32 L67-72).
- **Refusing the confluence frees the slot for a better one.** Rufus: "I promise you that is not the way to go" (Book 1, Ch21 L57).
- **A confluence guarantees a role.** Clive taught Jason to shape a power set "by aiming for powers within a certain scope" rather than seeking specific powers (Book 4, Ch65 L29), and the same confluence plays differently by user (see section 3).

### 6. Changes over time

---

## Magic on Earth

### 1. Summary

### 2. How it works

**The old magic.** Craig Vermillion: "Magic has always been a difficult and esoteric thing. Some five centuries ago, a new kind of magic appeared. People with no connection to the old ways could suddenly wield a variety of easy-to-use mystical powers. At that time, they were a limited threat. They were collected into various secret societies around the world, hoarding their knowledge" (Book 4, Ch11 L4).

**Tiers.** The shared numeric rank system runs from zero up: "Whether you call them tiers, categories, realms, or ranks, like you, the same numbers are recognised across the board" (Book 4, Ch11 L8). Tier zero is people without enough magic to "cross the first, transformative threshold" (Book 4, Ch11 L10). Blood servants can reach the first or second tier temporarily by ingesting vampire blood (Book 4, Ch11 L14). Vermillion says he and Jason are "tier two of five", that he has heard of tier-four Cabal figures who "entered forms of hibernation, hidden away for centuries", and that the fifth tier is "the limit of mortal power" (Book 4, Ch11 L19-22). In Network usage the ranks are called categories after spirit coin colours: category one is iron, two bronze, three silver (Book 4, Ch21 L33; Book 4, Ch31 L48). See `Ranks_and_Advancement.md`.

**The Grid.** The Grid is the Network's planet-wide monitor of dimensional incursions. "The grid infrastructure isn't like a normal formation array of permanent ritual circles. Each node is enormous and not made from a ritual circle at all. It's like the landscape is somehow operating as a series of interconnected rituals, creating a magical array from the natural features of the world itself. We're talking about nodes the size of cities, with elements made up of mountains, hills and rivers to form a magical array covering the entire planet" (Book 5, Ch5 L11). Its "express purpose is to identify and monitor dimensional spaces" (Book 4, Ch51 L35). It is "designed to be accessible to anyone with the requisite knowledge", so that once Jason knows about it he may tap into it himself (Book 4, Ch43 L33). It registers events by category: an incident in Book 4 "registered as being above category five" (Book 4, Ch5 L13). A local branch once hid the Saint-Étienne astral space by disabling the Grid in that area and "claiming that there was an infrastructure collapse" (Book 4, Ch51 L35).

**Where magic comes from.** Dawn explains: "The link between worlds ... It's been siphoning off magic from Pallimustus to this world for centuries. It was slow, at first, but the rate of transfer has been rapidly escalating over the last century and a half. Each proto-space that breaks down without the anchor creatures being destroyed deposits its magic into your world... after centuries, the magical density of your world has started to rise. This strengthens the link, which feeds the loop" (Book 4, Ch68 L59-61). The founders of the Grid and the Network "failed to prepare a response to proto-spaces appearing deep in the oceans", so the Network "slows the rate at which your world's magical density has risen" without stopping it (Book 4, Ch68 L63-65). See `Astral_Spaces_Zones_and_Surges.md` for the proto-spaces themselves.

**Supplies.** Earth has no spirit coin farms: "Earth doesn't have the magic and coin formation takes months, so you can't do it in the proto-astral spaces" (Book 4, Ch37 L79). Quintessence is called affinity gems: "We call them affinity gems. I'm pretty sure the Network is the main supplier" (Book 4, Ch23 L26). The Network gives its executive staff essences to reach category one and reserves cores for frontline staff (Book 4, Ch36 L77). Magical technology exists, built on leaning "on the magic as little as possible. Let the technology do the work and use magic to skip over the places where the tech would otherwise bottleneck" (Book 4, Ch46 L90).

**Characters on Earth.** Essence users on Earth are mostly category one to three in Book 4 (Book 4, Ch16 L108; Book 4, Ch18 L122). Vampires are the Cabal's classic power; the Engineers of Ascension are a radical group (see `05_Organisations/Earth_Powers.md`).

### 3. Variations and exceptions

- **Low-magic rule.** Entering the vicinity of a proto-astral aperture raises ambient magic enough that "your recovery rates will remain at normal levels without spirit coin consumption" (Book 4, Ch39 L55); outside such places Earth's magic is thin.
- **Core use as policy.** Network members are given cores according to role and rank rather than by ability (Book 4, Ch36 L77).
- **Training divergence.** The Network's own training is human-derived; Jason and Farrah introduce training "from the ground up" in Book 5 (Book 5, Ch1 L51-53).

### 4. Examples from the books

- **The Starlight Rider and the Network's tactical teams** (see Book 4 timeline).
- **Kostroma**, the first transformation zone, in Book 5, Ch44 L48.
- **Nitra, Slovakia**, a zone at the end of Book 5 (Book 5, Ch68 L68).

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **Earth has no magic.** It has a trickle, increasingly large (Book 4, Ch68 L59-61).
- **The Network can contain it.** Jason concludes that the Network's mission of containment "was completely stuffed from the start" because the oceans hide leaks, and Dawn agrees (Book 4, Ch68 L63-65).
- **Tier five is a myth.** Vermillion has "never seen proof" of it (Book 4, Ch11 L21).

### 6. Changes over time

- **Level 2:** In Book 5, grid failures and monster waves begin (Book 5, Ch14 L3; Book 5, Ch16 L78); the Network becomes publicly known as the Global Defense Network (Book 5, Ch17 L7). Zones such as Kostroma remake places (Book 5, Ch44 L48-52).

---

## Essence Catalogue

### 1. Summary

The books name far more essences than the wiki lists give with evidence. This file collects those the text actually shows, with who holds them and what the text says of their behaviour. Rarity statements come from the text; where the text gives none, none is invented. It is intended as a quick reference for building a character's power set, not as a mechanical list. The full set of essences the Magic Society knows is in its living document (Book 1, Ch20 L97).

The wiki's fuller lists of essences, confluences and combinations are in `../Appendices/Wiki_Reference_Lists.md` (wiki tier), with a cross-reference to the first book line for each entry found in the text. Where the wiki gives a rarity, it agrees with the text so far: sin is Legendary, renewal is Epic (the text's "second-highest rarity", Book 6, Ch13 L68) and rune and vast are among the highest.

### 2. How to read it

- An **essence** is named here by its concept. A **confluence** is a fourth essence produced from three others (see `Confluences.md`).
- "Seen with" lists a person or group for whom the text names the essence and gives a line.
- Rarity is only given where the text gives it. The text uses five stages for stones: common, uncommon, rare, epic, legendary (Book 2, Ch9 L9; Book 2, Ch9 L17 puts four-star at the "second highest rarity"). Essences are discussed with the same words.

### 3. Rarity as the text gives it

| Essence | What the text says |
|---|---|
| Animal essences (bear, wolf, snake, rat, and others) | "Common, as much as any essences were common"; bear, wolf and snake "quite prized", rat less so (Book 1, Ch73 L49) |
| Might, shield, magic | Common and desirable, "could all have been traded for some quality awakening stones" (Book 1, Ch73 L51) |
| Gathering | A rare essence (Book 4, Ch21 L135) |
| Growth, life | Uncommon (Book 4, Ch2 L18) |
| Rune, vast | Among the highest-rarity essences (Book 4, Ch64 L70) |
| Renewal | "Second-highest rarity", but "as valuable as most legendary essences" because it is the premier essence for healers (Book 6, Ch13 L68) |
| Wing | Highly sought after, used in very desirable combinations; much rarer (Book 4, Ch65 L28; Book 6, Ch13 L69) |
| Sin | "The rarest category" (Book 1, Ch20 L104) |
| Death | Restricted by itself (Book 1, Ch20 L42-44) |

### 4. Essences the books attest

The order follows first appearance in the series within each group.

#### Jason Asano's set

| Essence | Notes |
|---|---|
| Dark | First essence Jason uses; it turns to smoke and invades his body, and he passes out (Book 1, Ch21 L3). Bonded to Speed (Book 1, Ch99 L67-70). Gives Path of Shadows (Book 1, Ch22 L114) |
| Blood | A red cube, wet-looking but dry and warm (Book 1, Ch19 L68). Self-healing is "very common" with blood, and its abilities "tend to be more insidious. Bleeding, poison" (Book 1, Ch19 L75). Bonded to Power (Book 1, Ch21 L6). Gives Blood Harvest and Leech Bite (Book 1, Ch21 L7; Ch22 L72) |
| Sin | White and gold cube; "an essence of transgression" (Book 1, Ch20 L49-50). Bonded to Recovery (Book 1, Ch21 L49). Gives Punish, Feast of Absolution, Sin Eater (Book 1, Ch21 L50; Ch22 L84-85). Jason says it "isn't about defying gods" for him (Book 3, Ch8 L38) |
| Doom (confluence) | Bonded to Spirit (Book 1, Ch21 L61). Gives Inexorable Doom (Book 1, Ch21 L62) |

#### The first team and Greenstone circle

| Essence | Seen with |
|---|---|
| Fire, earth, potent, volcano | Farrah Hurin (Book 1, Ch19 L30) |

### 5. Variations and misconceptions

- **Names and concepts.** An essence name is a concept, not a promise of power: the bird essence might give "the power to talk to birds" rather than a combat power (Book 4, Ch65 L27).
- **Wiki lists.** The fan wiki grades many essences by rarity; those grades are not used here when the text gives a different one. Where the text and the wiki conflict, the text is followed (for example renewal, above).

## Essences

### 1. Summary

An essence is the most powerful and rarest of the forms in which magic condenses into something that is not alive. It is absorbed into a person's soul and gives that person a set of five abilities tied to its concept. Four essences make a full set. A person with a full set becomes iron rank and is changed by it. Essences are how Pallimustus makes essence users: the people who fight monsters, serve in the churches and run most of the world's institutions.

### 2. How it works

**What an essence is.** Rufus lists three non-living manifestations of magic: essences, "the most powerful manifestation" and "also, the rarest", then quintessence, "kind of like chunks of essence", and awakening stones (Book 1, Ch19 L45-56). A pile of quintessence cannot be used as an essence; "there's always some crackpot who claims to have figured it out, but it isn't possible" (Book 1, Ch19 L48-50). Clive's explanation is that the astral is "the source of all magic", that some worlds soak up a lot of it, and that this world's magic takes forms such as essences, stones, quintessence and monsters (Book 1, Ch69 L99). Essences appear as cubes or crystals: Jason's blood essence is a red cube whose slick surface looks wet but is dry and warm (Book 1, Ch19 L68); his sin essence is a white and gold cube (Book 1, Ch21 L44).

**Where they come from.** Magic "just starts manifesting all over the place" in a world as rich in it as this one (Book 1, Ch19 L33). They are also bought and sold. Jason trades a rat essence to a broker for two awakening stones (Book 1, Ch87 L91), brokers run regular essence and stone auctions (Book 1, Ch113 L61; Book 2, Ch70 L30), and after the Book 2 expedition the market price of essences and stones reached "an all-time low" (Book 3, Ch1 L100). Families pool money to buy essence sets for promising young people, and criminals buy powerful ones (Book 3, Ch1 L101). Dean's family could "afford a full set of essences" for him from a living made with water quintessence (Book 1, Ch96 L18). Gods can also supply essences (section 3).

**Absorption.** Absorbing an essence is done by an essence ritual (Book 1, Ch15 L113). Farrah offers to perform one for Jason (Book 1, Ch15 L112), and a ritual is ordinarily required for stones too (Book 1, Ch22 L38-41). The prompt that follows asks whether to absorb and reports "You have absorbed 2 of 4 essences" (Book 1, Ch21 L4). Absorption is painful. Jason passes out for the first (the dark essence turns to smoke and invades his body), the blood essence melts over his hand and burns into his flesh (Book 1, Ch21 L3), and the sin essence fills him like inflating pressure (Book 1, Ch21 L46). Farrah observes that each absorption seems easier and asks whether subjects adapt (Book 1, Ch21 L10-11). Once absorbed, "an essence cannot be relinquished or replaced" (Book 1, Ch20 L53).

**Five abilities, one awakened.** Each essence "imbues 1 awakened essence ability and 4 unawakened essence abilities" (Book 1, Ch20 L52). A full set therefore has twenty abilities, and a person "can't even properly progress towards bronze rank until you awaken every essence ability" (Book 1, Ch22 L68).

**Attribute bonding.** Each essence bonds to one attribute and raises it from normal to iron: blood to Power, dark to Speed, sin to Recovery and doom to Spirit in Jason's case (Book 1, Ch21 L6, L49, L61). Mastering all of an essence's abilities then raises that attribute (Book 1, Ch21 L6).

**Order of use.** Farrah and Rufus advise using all essences first and stones afterwards, because abilities are shaped by the essences and abilities a person already has; "awaken too many abilities before you have all your essences, and your abilities will lack focus" (Book 1, Ch21 L36-37).

**The fourth essence.** The first three are chosen; the fourth is a confluence and arrives by itself (Book 1, Ch19 L30). See `Confluences.md`.

**Iron rank.** The fourth essence completes the set. Jason's message lists the changes: damage reduction against normal-rank sources, increased resistance to normal-rank effects, the ability to sense auras, and the ability to sustain himself with concentrated magic (Book 1, Ch21 L65-70). The body expels impurities as it moves toward "a state of perfection" (Book 1, Ch22 L9). See `Ranks_and_Advancement.md`.

### 3. Variations and exceptions

**Outworlder absorption.** Jason absorbed his first essence without using a ritual ("I just kind of absorbed it") and thinks it was tied to the same power that lets him speak every language (Book 1, Ch15 L114). The ritual knowledge from the skill book tells him a ritual is normally required, "except, he knew from experience that wasn't always true" (Book 1, Ch15 L113).

**Racial difference.** Humans "have their essence abilities increase faster than other races" (Book 1, Ch15 L58). Abilities and their awakening differ by person as well as race (Book 1, Ch15 L57).

**Divine essences.** Gods give their clergy "divine versions of regular essences", so clergy reject the confluence (Book 1, Ch21 L57). The Healer's church supplies the essences and stones for its healing abilities (Book 1, Ch35 L23). Divine essences come back to the god: when the Builder cult's captives are freed, those with divine essences have the power "returned to their god" and are weaker (Book 3, Ch61 L11). A freed group of cult-held priests who lose divine gifts either join another church or replace them with regular essences (Book 2, Ch64 L124-126). Ex-clergy stripped of divinely gifted essences suffer "a debilitating imbalance in the body and soul" (Book 4, Ch11 L16). Jason refuses divine essences because "the idea of some god repossessing my magic powers doesn't appeal" (Book 2, Ch56 L93); Clive adds that great astral beings cannot make essences "the way that gods can" but can make their own stones (Book 2, Ch56 L92).

**Restricted essences.** The Adventure Society keeps a list of essences and combinations "that pose an inherent threat to ordinary people", with the death essence at the top of it by itself, "because it always seems to go wrong" (Book 1, Ch20 L42-44). Membership is impossible with a restricted essence (Book 1, Ch20 L44). The combinations that produce the undeath confluence almost all involve death (Book 1, Ch20 L19). The Magic Society's living document lists every known essence and combination, with restricted ones flagged, and Jason's sin essence is in "the rarest category" (Book 1, Ch20 L97-104).

**Rarity.** Essences vary in how often they appear and how common they are. Farrah says the sin essence is in the rarest category (Book 1, Ch20 L104). A rune essence reaches Clive as an "epic four-star essence" (Book 2, Ch9 L21). Low-rarity essences are the ones ordinary buyers afford (Book 1, Ch108 L28). The rat essence is "not popular with anyone who could afford to be choosy" (Book 1, Ch87 L91).

**Not all essences are simple.** An essence can seem to carry a meaning far from its name: Neil says it takes arrogance to absorb "the essence of defying the gods", and Jason replies that the sin essence is not about defying gods for him (Book 3, Ch8 L37-44).

### 4. Examples from the books

- Jason: dark, blood and sin, giving the doom confluence (Book 1, Ch20 L110; Ch21 L54-62).
- Farrah: fire, earth and potent, giving volcano (Book 1, Ch19 L30).
- Sophie: swift, balance and wind, giving mystic (Book 2, Ch5 L82-87).
- Belinda: magic, trap and adept, giving charlatan (Book 2, Ch32 L67-72).
- Clive: magic, rune and balance, giving karmic (Book 2, Ch28 L56).
- Neil: shield, growth and renewal, giving prosperity (Book 2, Ch28 L44).

The full list of essences and who holds them is in `Essence_Catalogue.md`.

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **Essences change who you are.** Anisa's church holds that "the essences we use change who we are" and favours only the ones it sees as holy or pure, saying other essences taint the soul (Book 1, Ch20 L84). Rufus's reply is that essence abilities "aren't inherently good or bad. Like a sword, they can be used to oppress or protect"; accountability lies with the wielder (Book 1, Ch20 L86). He allows that the restricted list covers "rare and extreme cases" (Book 1, Ch20 L89). An American religious type told Jason "Essences are god's test to see who is worthy of the power" (Book 4, Ch43 L10).
- **Rarer means better.** For stones, Rufus says "rare stones don't give out better abilities, just more specific ones" (Book 1, Ch19 L66); the same applies in spirit to essences, whose power comes from concept and user.
- **You can make an essence from quintessence.** Farrah calls it impossible (Book 1, Ch19 L50).
- **A confluence can be found loose.** "You can't find a volcano essence anywhere" (Book 1, Ch19 L31).

### 6. Later developments

- **Level 2:** Jason offers awakening stones to former Engineers of Ascension members, saying there are enough in his stock to send two of them off "with a full set of powers", and notes that rushing is not ideal (Book 6, Ch26 L97).

## Familiars, Summons and Conjurations

### 1. Summary

A familiar is a creature made or called to serve one person for life. A summoned familiar is built by ritual from magical materials and a being drawn from the deep astral; a bonded familiar is a creature that answers a call. Familiars are different from the short-lived summons many essence users can conjure in the middle of a fight, and from conjurations, which are things drawn out of mana. All three are essence abilities and come from awakening stones or from the essences themselves; none can be used by anyone who lacks the necessary ritual knowledge.

### 2. How it works

**What a summoned familiar is.** Clive's explanation: "Summoning a familiar is like deliberately creating a monster. A chunk of magic is brought into our world and forms a body. What makes it different from a monster is that it also summons a creature from the deep astral. Such entities are purely magical, like a soul. They normally can't exist in physical reality, any more than we can exist in the astral. But they inhabit the body you've made. Give it a mind, and stability. So it doesn't break down and go berserk" (Book 1, Ch69 L127). An outworlder's body is akin to a monster's or a summoned familiar's: "physical substance forged out of raw magic" (Book 2, Ch25 L59). Summoned familiars from the deep astral are "an endless supply of bizarre and terrifying horrors" (Book 2, Ch64 L15).

**Summoning versus calling.** Farrah explains the two kinds. A summoning "is a more elaborate ritual. Unlike a regular summoning, you should only need it each time you go up a rank. Unless your familiar gets killed, in which case you'll have to summon it back." A call-type power works differently: "a creature that has an affinity to that call will come and form a bond with the person. Less costly than summoning, but if that kind of familiar dies, you can't just summon it again" (Book 1, Ch23 L42-44).

**Summoned versus bonded.** A bonded familiar survives its owner's death; a summoned one does not, but a summoned familiar "can disappear into the summoner's body, which bonded familiars can't" (Book 1, Ch75 L27-28). A bonded familiar can be sensed at all times, a summoned one only while subsumed in the summoner (Book 3, Ch5 L71). Humphrey and his dragon Stash "could always sense one another... over any distance" (Book 2, Ch42 L70). Bonding is more reliable: "when going for a bonded familiar instead of a summoned one, things are much more likely to go your way, if you prepare accordingly", and "as close as you'll come to a guaranteed result with any awakening stone" (Book 1, Ch75 L33).

**The ritual.** To summon Jason's first familiar, he draws the circle with blood and magical materials while the rest watch; the circle and the pool of blood vanish into the floor "drawn back through the astral channel created by the summoning" (Book 1, Ch23 L61-70). Each rank-up needs a resummoning with materials suited to the new rank: Jason brings bronze-rank materials in advance and two sets for each familiar in case a vessel is destroyed (Book 3, Ch14 L78). The text lists a requirement for Shade's bronze resummoning as 343 dark quintessence gems (Book 2, Ch63 L92). Other circles can be simple: "Most people just need a circle of good old salt" (Book 1, Ch93 L41).

**Who can have one.** "You can only awaken that kind of essence ability if you already understand ritual magic" (Book 1, Ch23 L37). Jason's ritual skill book gave "the bare minimum" to be a proper ritual magician and is "how you awakened a familiar summoning power" (Book 1, Ch23 L36). Rufus has no summoning power because his obsession is swordsmanship (Book 1, Ch23 L35-37).

**Subsuming.** A summoned familiar "can temporarily disperse its body and place its spirit inside the summoner" (Book 1, Ch23 L82). Belinda's lantern familiar Shimmer sits in her eyes, turning them silver; purple eyes mark another's summoned familiar (Book 3, Ch5 L69). Belinda's familiar can see hidden enemies from her eyes and fire disruptive-force beams from them (Book 2, Ch72 L57).

**Summons that are not familiars.** A temporary summon "was not a summoned familiar but a temporary summons, like Gary's forge golem or Farrah's magma elemental. It would only last for a limited time, but he could afford to risk it in ways that he couldn't with a familiar" (Book 2, Ch28 L46). Farrah and Gary "call up short-lived monsters" as essence powers, and "Rituals that are also essence powers tend to be quick and dirty" (Book 1, Ch23 L38-39).

### 3. Variations and exceptions

- **Swarm familiars are rare.** Rick: "Swarm-type familiars are really rare. I've seen more dragon and phoenix familiars" (Book 1, Ch68 L22).
- **Many familiars.** Henrietta keeps three summoned familiars inside her body and a fourth, a phoenix, bonded (Book 3, Ch5 L70). Jason and Clive each have summoned familiars (Book 2, Ch28 L48).
- **Appearance echoes the owner.** Clive notes that Jason's two familiars "are both reflective of your appearance" in his cloak (Book 2, Ch72 L133).
- **Apocalypse beast familiars.** Jason's first, the Sanguine Horror, is described as "an apocalypse beast", and Clive notes Jason "is not the only adventurer to have an apocalypse beast familiar" (Book 2, Ch63 L100; Book 3, Ch8 L33).
- **Powers not tied to a familiar.** Neil's summon "would only last for a limited time" and works best when fighting alone (Book 2, Ch28 L46).

### 4. Examples from the books

- **Humphrey's dragon Stash.** A bonded familiar (Book 2, Ch42 L70).
- **Belinda's lantern.** A summoned astral lantern familiar called Shimmer (Book 2, Ch71 L50; Book 3, Ch5 L69).
- **Henrietta's phoenix.** The only bonded familiar among four she has, "rare and elusive" (Book 3, Ch5 L70).

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **A familiar will eat you.** When Colin's swarm crawls into Jason's wounds, Gary asks if it is eating him; Farrah says, "It's his familiar" (Book 1, Ch23 L74-76).
- **Summoned means disposable.** It costs ritual materials at each rank-up (Book 3, Ch14 L78).
- **Bonded is better.** Jason's summoned familiars "had many advantages over a bonded familiar like Humphrey's, but a bond had its own advantages" (Book 2, Ch42 L70).

### 6. Changes over time

- **Level 1:** In Book 2 Jason awakens Shadow of the Reaper and Hand of the Reaper from stones of the Reaper (Book 2, Ch63 L73, L115) and Avatar of Doom as his final doom ability (Book 2, Ch64 L5).

---

## Monster Cores and Training

### 1. Summary

An essence user can grow in power in two ways. One is to consume monster cores, which is easy and can be bought. The other is to train, fight real danger and meditate. Rufus's teaching in Book 1 is that the first method eats into the potential of the second. The setting's politics, from the aristocratic families of Greenstone to the Network on Earth, turn on which method a group has used, and on what it costs them later.

### 2. How it works

**Cores.** A monster core is a teardrop-shaped crystal dropped by a monster, red and more vibrant for higher ranks. It is "the magic core of an iron rank monster (crafting material, magic core)" (Book 1, Ch29 L79-81). The same cores are crafting material and the means of advancing. Lesser cores come from the lowest monsters (Book 1, Ch2 L32); iron, bronze and higher are labelled by rank (Book 1, Ch27 L94; Book 1, Ch99 L155).

**What a core does.** Rufus: "Every core increases your abilities a little, but only a little. It takes hundreds of iron-rank cores to reach bronze rank, and that's for humans. For everyone else, it takes even more. It takes iron-rank cores when you're iron rank, bronze when you're bronze, and so forth" (Book 1, Ch31 L96).

**What a core costs.** "Every time you use a monster core to raise your abilities, it makes the other method a little less effective. The impact is minimal, at first, but every core you use eats into your potential. If you used cores to get to where I am, the top end of bronze rank, then cores would the only thing that works anymore" (Book 1, Ch31 L98). Farrah describes the feel: "if you use magic cores to advance, you get to sleep in" (Book 1, Ch39 L30). The magic inside a person "feels unruly ... Like some wild creature", and for core users it is "like feeding the beast drugged meat to make it compliant. The beast still has its strength, but the owner can't make use of it properly" (Book 1, Ch31 L126-130).

**Who uses cores.** "In the city we're going to, Greenstone, almost everyone uses cores. So long as you have the money to buy them you can reach bronze rank without ever facing a monster. But every rank requires more and more cores. By the time you reach silver rank, things slow right down as the costs go up significantly. Most core users don't make it to gold" (Book 1, Ch31 L100). Most aristocratic families "only have a few truly powerful adventurers, while the rest use cores" (Book 1, Ch31 L102). Arena fighters rank up on cores, but the professionals "stop using cores before they hit gold" because "getting to gold rank using monster cores is a tough ask"; being at the peak of silver "keeps them at the top of their game" (Book 2, Ch73 L8-12). A grandmother taken in by a protective friend is given "money, essences and enough monster cores to rank her up to bronze" (Book 2, Ch64 L151).

**The real method.** Rufus lists three elements (Book 1, Ch31 L108-122):
1. **Training.** Push every attribute to its limits, not just physical ones: observation, memory games and puzzles train perception, "part of your spiritual strength" (Book 1, Ch31 L110-114).
2. **Fighting monsters.** "To truly break through your limits, you must truly push up against them. Only with genuine danger can you go further and do more than you ever thought possible" (Book 1, Ch31 L118).
3. **Meditation.** "The other two elements are about breaking through your own limits. Meditation is about consolidating that gain. It's where you take the fleeting moments in which you were better than you've ever been before, and make that your new normal" (Book 1, Ch31 L122).

**Aura training.** Farrah adds aura training, since "your chances of getting to gold start with a good foundation, which means aura powers and aura training" (Book 1, Ch40 L48). See `Auras.md`.

**Seeing taint.** An aura with heavy core use can be read. Jason's assessment of a silver-rank necromancer shows "Essence ability advancement impediment (monster core taint): 94%" (Book 6, Ch39 L27). Outworlders in Rimaros "carried a heavy taint of monster core use in their auras, including all of the silver-rankers", while bronze-rankers without it "showed more evidence of proper training with their aura control" (Book 7, Ch37 L4).

### 3. Variations and exceptions

- **Policy rather than preference.** Anna and Asya had never used cores, but "that was a matter of policy": Network executives got essences to raise them to category one, while cores were saved for frontline enforcement staff, and only committee members were raised to category two with cores (Book 4, Ch36 L77).
- **A rare non-core option.** The Network has a member who refuses cores and took eight years to reach category two (Book 4, Ch18 L36).
- **Core users with skill.** Akari, a core user, warns that "the danger is in letting them be the only source of your strength" and notes there are more core users whose skill fails their power than those who reach their potential (Book 5, Ch25 L6-8).
- **Forced cores.** The Order of Redeeming Light implants purified cores, forcibly if needed. The process "rendered essences unusable, replacing their powers with lesser and not entirely predictable alternatives" yet raised the implanted person "an entire rank" (Book 8, Ch9 L35-36), and the cores "commanded the absolute obedience" of those implanted (Book 8, Ch9 L37).
- **Earth's training.** The Network and the US and Chinese governments already used variants of Farrah's training programme, "for as much as a century" in the case of the latter two (Book 5, Ch25 L10). Jason's enemies in a Book 5 fight had "clearly received meticulous combat training" derived from human methods, with powers "incorporated as an addendum", which wastes the potential of a bronze-rank body (Book 5, Ch1 L51-52). Jason had been "trained as an essence user from the ground up", with "improved meditation techniques to get people off cores" (Book 5, Ch1 L53).

### 4. Examples from the books

- **Jason and Farrah's programme.** Introduced to the Network as a holistic method of going from "ordinary warrior to magical weapon" (Book 5, Ch1 L53). Asya's frustration with being the only category one on her flight crew, because core users around her rank faster, shows the cost of training the right way (Book 5, Ch25 L5).
- **Todd 'The Necromancer' Halverson.** A silver-ranker with 94 percent taint (Book 6, Ch39 L25-27).
- **Miles Cotezee.** A senior administrator whose silver rank is "entirely from cores" (Book 6, Ch47 L878).

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **Cores are cheating.** Akari: "There is no shame in using monster cores to grow. The danger is in letting them be the only source of your strength" (Book 5, Ch25 L6).
- **Cores are free power.** They cost potential (Book 1, Ch31 L98).
- **Training can be skipped by wealth.** Rufus's point is that wealth buys bronze, not the ability to use it (Book 1, Ch31 L100, L130).

### 6. Changes over time

- **Level 2:** By Book 7 the Network's outworlders in Rimaros are mostly core users in the silver rank (Book 7, Ch37 L4).

---

## Monsters

### 1. Summary

Monsters are the most common way magic manifests in a magic-rich world. They appear from nothing, usually in the wild, live for a time that depends on their rank, and then dissolve back into raw magic if not killed first. They have ranks like essence users; the ranks are not given by essences but by the density of the magic that makes them. Hunting them before they reach their aggressive final stage is the bulk of an adventurer's work, and what they leave behind funds much of the economy.

### 2. How it works

**Where they come from.** Rufus: "Most magic manifestations are monsters. They just appear, hopefully in the wilderness, but the magic they're made of isn't stable. Eventually they break down and dissolve back into magic. Killing them just makes it faster" (Book 1, Ch19 L34). The astral is "the source of all magic" and monsters are one of the forms the magic takes in a world that soaks up a lot of it (Book 1, Ch69 L99).

**Rank.** Monsters "exist within a certain power threshold. Whether an essence user or a monster, each rank has a suppressive effect on lower ranks" (Book 1, Ch19 L42). They do not use essences (Book 1, Ch19 L41-42). Rank "was a function of their magical density, not actual power": the strongest iron-rank monsters are stronger than the weakest bronze ones, with the difference usually made up in numbers (Book 1, Ch84 L57).

**Lifespan and aggression.** "Lesser monsters only last a week or two. They start so close to breaking down that they're aggressive from the moment they appear, but they aren't really a threat. An old woman with a broom can handle them. Iron rank monsters last about a month, getting aggressive in the final week or so. It goes up from there" (Book 1, Ch19 L40). "Monsters frequently aren't a problem when they first manifest, but as they get closer to breaking down they become highly aggressive. The bulk of our job as adventurers is hunting them down before they reach that stage" (Book 1, Ch19 L36). A region that has gone without hunters for two months will have iron-rank monsters in their berserk stage, while bronze monsters that spawned in that period will not have reached it (Book 3, Ch7 L5).

**Dissolution and loot.** A dead monster "dissolved into rainbow smoke" (Book 1, Ch32 L40). Anything it spilled dissolves too, and the smoke can harm a person who gets its fluids in the mouth (Book 1, Ch27 L91). The loot is whatever it leaves behind: monster cores, spirit coins, materials and the occasional essence or stone (Book 1, Ch27 L94-96; Book 1, Ch73 L46-51). One blood weaver produced three essences at once, an event "less common than even a legendary essence appearing" (Book 4, Ch21 L136). A monster that is not a monster but an animal does not dissolve (Book 1, Ch19 L35-36).

**Where they are strong.** Magic density sets the usual rank: around Greenstone "iron-rank monsters are the norm, with a good smattering of bronze-rank. Silvers can show up, but only very occasionally" (Book 1, Ch30 L43). In Vitesse's region "you get more silver rank monsters than anything. You see as many golds as you do bronze, and sometimes even diamond rank monsters" (Book 1, Ch30 L44). In a saturated place monsters hunt in packs: "even normally solitary monsters appeared in packs" (Book 2, Ch53 L29). A silver-rank monster can linger for years before breaking down in a low-magic area (Book 2, Ch43 L56). Diamond monsters usually come only in surges (Book 7, Ch10 L19).

**Rank-up rule of thumb.** "A good adventurer should be able to handle monsters one rank up, so long as the match-up is good" (Book 2, Ch47 L59).

### 3. Variations and exceptions

- **Constructs and golems.** Siege golems and world engineers are constructs, not spawned monsters in the ordinary sense; world engineers are "diamond-rank golems larger than most buildings" (Book 8, Ch6 L21).
- **Astral entities.** Nightmare hags are "diamond-rank entities from the astral that have no physical existence in this place and cannot harm you directly" (Book 2, Ch57 L68).
- **Anomalies.** In the transformation zones, monsters called anomalies (living, unliving, reality-dysphoric, greater) appear (Book 6, Ch2 L10; Book 6, Ch8 L73; Book 6, Ch11 L43; Book 6, Ch15 L4).
- **Apocalypse beasts.** A creature "made from alchemy, blood and things best left unmentioned" can be a sanguine horror, an apocalypse monster that Jason says "can scour a world of life" (Book 1, Ch12 L70; Book 1, Ch71 L65).
- **Monsters as familiars.** A familiar is made like a deliberate monster, with a creature from the deep astral inhabiting it (Book 1, Ch69 L127).

The fan wiki's rank pages list further monsters by rank (wiki); they are copied, tier-marked, into `../Appendices/Wiki_Reference_Lists.md`, section 4.

### 5. Common misconceptions in-world

- **Monsters are evil.** They are manifestations of unstable magic that dissolves; Rufus dislikes people who relish killing "everyone you find" (Book 1, Ch19 L37-38).
- **Rank equals strength.** Rank is magical density, not power (Book 1, Ch84 L57).
- **Animals and monsters are alike.** A snake that is an animal does not dissolve like a monster (Book 1, Ch19 L35).

### 6. Changes over time

- **Level 2:** The haul from the Book 3 astral space is described in Book 4 as many duplicate essences from the same monsters: half plant essences, the rest mostly venom, might and animal essences (Book 4, Ch2 L18).
- **Level 2:** Jason defeats a silver-rank living anomaly of the kind a transformation zone produces (Book 6, Ch2 L10, L31).

For Jason's full gold-rank power set, see Jason_Power_Set_Reference.md. "First shown" means the first line the compendium found, which may come a chapter after an unreported awakening.
