## Daily Life: Calendar, Food, Clothing and Transport

### 1. Summary

The practical texture of life in Greenstone and the delta: the calendar, what people wear and eat, how they travel and what runs on spirit coins.

### 2. Facts

- **Calendar**: "twelve months of thirty days, divided up into early, mid and late stages of each season", five extra days for the solstices, equinoxes and the new year, which falls at the beginning of spring (Book 1, Ch22 L19). The week has six days (Book 6, Ch10 L41).
- **Clothing**: "Most people, whether in Greenstone or the delta, wore loose-fitting, breathable clothes because of the heat"; adventurers on a job wear fitted outfits, often with protective properties (Book 1, Ch59 L30). Elf fashion has flowing, tapered lines (Book 9, Ch46 L5).
- **Food**: preparation is often familiar, with bread, soup, sandwiches and cake, but the ingredients are mostly different because farms raise different animals and trees bear different fruit (Book 1, Ch45 L8). Jason lists local sauce ingredients with a village cook (Book 1, Ch25 L13).
- **Housing**: Leust, one of the delta's largest towns, has paved roads and stone buildings "instead of mud-brick" (Book 1, Ch93 L5); in the Island, green stone appears everywhere (Book 1, Ch45 L5).
- **Transport**: heidels are "the size and shape of a horse, but with scales instead of hair and two heads, each with a horn" (Book 1, Ch17 L106); the Island's districts are linked by a subterranean, submarine transit line called the loop (Book 1, Ch43 L5), with terminals laid out like train stations (Book 1, Ch37 L83).
- **Devices**: lamps, showers and most household magic run on lesser spirit coins (Book 1, Ch80 L40).

### 3. Using this in a story

Heat, coin-run conveniences and a loop line make Greenstone feel modern; outside it, heidels, mud-brick and the sun set the pace.

---

## Draconians

### 1. Summary

Large, scaled humanoids who claim descent from dragons and whose homeland is the Dragon Lands.

### 2. Facts

- **Appearance**: "a member of the rare draconian race, larger even than male leonids and covered in glossy scales", the colour of dark leaves, green moving into purple (Book 2, Ch38 L29); Maximilian is the tallest person in Greenstone, with a long hairless head and glossy scales (Book 2, Ch39 L15). A draconian skull is distinctive enough to identify skeletons (Book 1, Ch86 L31).
- **Pride in descent**: draconians "took pride in their claimed dragon ancestry and often had issues with other races wielding the dragon essence" (Book 2, Ch39 L34).
- **Rarity**: draconians are rare in Greenstone, where Maximilian is the only one present at a gathering (Book 2, Ch39 L15).

### 3. Using this in a story

A draconian is a rare, imposing figure; mention of a dragon essence in their company is a social risk.

---

## Dreisil: The People's Holy Federation

### 1. Summary

Dreisil is the nation of Kurdansk, Sophie's birthplace. The text names it once and gives its full title. Kurdansk_Culture.md covers the life of the city and is the main file for what is known of the nation's people.

### 2. Facts

- **Name**: Kurdansk lies "in the People's Holy Federation of Dreisil" (Book 6, Ch47 L390).
- **Humphrey's view**: "The more they try to make a nation sound free and righteous, the more tyrannical and corrupt it is" (Book 6, Ch47 L392).
- **Prisons**: Kurdansk uses prisons rather than indenture as punishment (see Kurdansk_Culture.md).

### 3. Not in the text

The Federation's structure, ruler, religion and borders are not described in the lines read. Dreisil is named only in Book 6.

---

## Economy and Money

### 1. Summary

How money works on Pallimustus: spirit coins in six denominations that also power devices and sustain essence users, farms that make them, and who controls them.

### 2. Facts

- **Denominations**: "The basic coin of the realm was the lesser spirit coin. Iron spirit coins were worth a hundred lesser coins, used by bulk traders, adventurers and other members of the wealthy elite. After that, it was ten iron to the bronze, ten bronze to the silver and ten silver to the gold"; three gold coins are "worth three hundred thousand units of the basic currency" (Book 1, Ch61 L65).
- **Why the lesser coin is the basic unit**: higher-rank coins are "not very useful for everyday life"; almost all home and business magic devices, such as lamps and showers, run on lesser coins and higher ranks would burn them out, while larger infrastructure such as the loop line uses more powerful coins (Book 1, Ch80 L40-41).
- **Sustenance**: ranked people can live on spirit coins; Rufus says a bronze coin will sustain bronze-rank adventurers without stressing their bodies, so it is about not going over your limit (Book 1, Ch15 L92).
- **Farms**: spirit coins are made on farms using special moulds that cause magic to crystallise, in delicate, very stable ambient magic (Book 1, Ch80 L21).
- **Why Greenstone**: most areas have too high a magical density to make lesser coins; the smallest denomination they can manage is usually iron or even bronze, and Greenstone was founded because the low-magic desert is a rare place for low-end farms (Book 1, Ch80 L37, L42). A farm that makes a thousand lesser coins would make only ten iron coins or one bronze coin (Book 1, Ch80 L39).
- **Volume**: spirit coins are Greenstone's largest export, and the desert farms produce almost three percent of the lesser coins used worldwide; most farms are run by the Geller or Mercer families under Magic Society regulation (Book 1, Ch80 L27-28).
- **Hazards**: volatile weather, a monster spawning nearby or an adventurer using abilities can ruin a batch, so adventurers wait outside the walls (Book 1, Ch80 L43); there is no defence against a monster spawning too close to a farm, and the loss is absorbed (Book 1, Ch80 L44-45).
- **Control**: "The Magic Society manages all of that"; coin production is the main source of its political power and the reason it must stay politically neutral (Book 1, Ch80 L23). An unregistered farm must be found and shut down (Book 1, Ch42 L64).
- **Escorts**: adventurers escort shipments of coins from a farm back to the city (Book 1, Ch77 L51).
- **Prices**: armour for an iron-rank adventurer is priced in bronze coins, which is unusual (Book 1, Ch61 L114).
- **Wealth and rank**: Jason offers three gold coins as a starting payment (Book 1, Ch61 L63-65), and later funds Jory's alchemy facility (Book 3, Ch16 L81; the amount is not stated).
- **Permits and trade**: trade and work permits get a person into the Island's trade lanes; aristocrats, adventurers and residents use the wide lane (Book 1, Ch36 L71).
- **Guild rewards**: during a surge the contribution board adds incentive rewards above the action quota, and guilds earn the biggest rewards (Book 7, Ch12 L15-21).
- **Indenture as income**: criminals' indentures are sold or auctioned to recoup the cost of their crimes (Book 1, Ch92 L32; see Law_and_Indenture.md).

### 3. Using this in a story

Money in a scene is rank-shaped: lesser coins buy lamps and showers, iron coins buy equipment for the elite, and a gold coin can change a community's fortunes.

---

## Elves

### 1. Summary

Elves are powerful spellcasters whose healing magic is unparalleled.

### 2. Facts

- **Reputation**: "Elves are powerful spell casters and their skill with healing magic is unparalleled" (Book 1, Ch15 L50).
- **Aptitude**: the elven aptitude is for spells rather than the special attacks humans favour (Book 3, Ch1 L90).
- **Racial gift**: Neil's elf racial ability, Life Affinity, evolves into Life Guard (Book 2, Ch61 L145).
- **Local families**: Greenstone has local elf families; Lucian Lamprey is an elf banished there who is not one of them (Book 1, Ch42 L25-30), and Pochard Finn is an elf and a local (Book 1, Ch42 L30).
- **Elf fashion**: "flowing, tapered lines" (Book 9, Ch46 L5).
- **Predation**: cannibals in Book 1 discuss elves as food (Book 1, Ch3 L43; Book 1, Ch15 L51).

### 3. Using this in a story

Good for healers, spellcasters and long-lived local power brokers; elf aristocrats tend to hold municipal office.

---

## Estercost: Culture of a Kingdom of Aristocrats

### 1. Summary

A large, densely populated kingdom of old noble houses, garden towers and high-magic farmland, and the homeland from which Greenstone was colonised.

### 2. Facts

- **Capital and councils**: Cyrion is the capital, yet the Adventure Society's continental council sits in Vitesse, where the royal family keeps a tower as a palace (Book 6, Ch47 L604); in Book 7 the Cyrion branch takes over the site of the operation from Vitesse (Book 7, Ch43 L58).

### 3. Using this in a story

Estercost scenes are about old money, tall flowering towers and polished manners; its aristocrats are used to the law being on their side.

---

## Girlano: Culture of a Cartel Kingdom

### 1. Summary

Girlano is an inland nation south of the Storm Kingdom, ostensibly a kingdom but controlled by cartels that produce banned or heavily controlled substances. It has a reputation, and the text shows it only through reputation and a border city.

### 2. Facts

- **Rule**: "ostensibly a kingdom", it is "famously controlled by cartels known for producing substances that ranged from heavily controlled to outright banned"; the cartels keep power through under-the-table deals with powerful groups in neighbouring countries, which prop up a puppet government while their own governments pay lip service to suppression (Book 8, Ch24 L22-23).
- **Economy**: the cartels exploit the region's rare plants and minerals, and the nation is surrounded by strong public sanctions and border controls (Book 8, Ch24 L22).
- **Smuggling culture**: the border with the Storm Kingdom is jungle-covered hills with many semi-hidden smuggling paths to the shore (Book 8, Ch24 L26). The border city Casallini is a small city where the Adventure Society keeps a large force because it is a border city (Book 8, Ch25 L136; Book 8, Ch26 L39).
- **Neutral Society**: the Adventure Society ignores borders and politics and keeps monsters out of population centres, so long as its work is not interfered with (Book 8, Ch24 L24-25).
- **Reputation**: Liara calls it "a stain full of drug dealers and smugglers" (Book 8, Ch25 L120). Belinda finds it "famously sketchy" with "all the opportunities it offered to an enterprising and open-minded young lady" (Book 9, Ch26 L3).
- **Hired muscle**: the men who attack Sophie's delivery are local hires from Casallini (Book 8, Ch25 L119).

### 3. Not in the text

Its people, language, food, dress and religion are not described in the lines read.

---

## Goblins and Fairies: Zone-Transformed Peoples

### 1. Summary

Goblins and fairies appear as monsters and as people of Earth transformed by the zones; fairies are also a small hovering manifestation.

### 2. Facts

- **Goblins as monsters**: Jason jokes about killing ten goblins for an uncommon spear in the cannibals' cave (Book 1, Ch4 L26).
- **Transformed humans**: in West Canfield, Detroit, people were "turned into goblins and their homes into underground warrens", which became a lawless combat zone (Book 5, Ch54 L52).
- **Cabal beings**: the Cabal's mythological beings include centaurs, ogres, fairies and more (Book 5, Ch54 L55).
- **Elves, goblins and fairies**: people in a magic city were "turned into elves and goblins and fairies" (Book 6, Ch23 L91).
- **Fairy manifestation**: a small hovering fairy-like figure with an androgynous body bites Clive in Book 2 (Book 2, Ch26 L99).

- **Earth beings in the open**: as the Cabal operated in the open, "centaurs, ogres, fairies, and more" were spotted by the media and revealed to the world (Book 5, Ch54 L55). A Network operative expects the Cabal to be "myths and fairy tale creatures" (Book 5, Ch61 L52).
- **Zone-turned people**: Emi says people in a magic city "were all turned into elves and goblins and fairies", so they were let live there (Book 6, Ch23 L91). Jason's cloud city is described as a fairy tale kingdom (Book 6, Ch19 L40), but that is a style, not a people.

### 3. Using this in a story

Goblins and fairies are Earth-side cases; no native Pallimustus culture of either is established here.

---

## Humans

### 1. Summary

The most common people of Pallimustus and Earth's native people, with gifts that favour special attacks and fast essence advancement.

### 2. Facts

- **Racial gifts**: an affinity for special attacks, essences that advance more rapidly than others', and latent powers that adapt to the essences (Book 1, Ch85 L35). The latent gifts are dormant until awakened and then take a form based on the person's essences (Book 1, Ch38 L73).
- **Evolution**: Clive's blessing from the Celestial Book changes his human special attack affinity into a spell affinity (Book 1, Ch85 L22-26); his Human Ambition becomes Thirst for Knowledge and Humphrey's Special Attack Affinity becomes Attack of the Mirage Dragon (Book 2, Ch61 L144, L147). Taika's human abilities have been replaced with outworlder ones (Book 9, Ch33 L15).
- **Distribution**: the Greenstone region is human-dominated (Book 1, Ch15 L56).
- **Humans and outworlders**: an outworlder who has a child with another species produces offspring of that species, so a human and an outworlder have a human child (Book 5, Ch44 L72).
- **Lifespan**: essence users age slower; bronze-rankers can live well past a hundred, silvers double that and gold-rankers live for centuries (Book 1, Ch22 L58).

### 3. Using this in a story

Humans are the default people of the delta and of every Earth scene; their gifts reward specialised, skilled builds.

---

## Jayapura: Culture of a Pranay Trade Port

### 1. Summary

Jayapura is a northern-coast sea-trade port of Pranay and the birthplace of Hester. Pranay_Culture.md covers the land; this file gives Jayapura's own life: the mirage arenas as entertainment, floating discs, markets and the Mystic Quarter.

### 2. Facts

- **Trade port**: one of the important sea-trade ports on Pranay's northern coast (Book 2, Ch69 L65-66).
- **Arena culture**: its mirage chamber complex is the city's premier entertainment venue, with essence users facing each other or illusion challenges before a paying audience (Book 2, Ch73 L4). "There are whole essence user teams who never become adventurers"; they earn money in the arena and use monster cores to rank (Book 2, Ch73 L7-8). Two leagues a year in silver, bronze and iron divisions lead to grand championships (Book 2, Ch73 L10). See Mirage_Chamber_Association.md.
- **Retirement**: arena professionals stop using cores before gold, retire when silver longevity runs out and become gold-rank managers of the arena (Book 2, Ch73 L12-14).
- **Layout**: a flat circular arena building at the edge of the Mystic Quarter, spilling into the theatre district (Book 2, Ch73 L6).
- **Transport**: those who can afford them ride platter-sized floating discs (Book 2, Ch70 L3); floating discs are sold in the Mystic Quarter (Book 3, Ch5 L83).
- **Markets and food**: the team visits its markets and finds new customs and food (Book 3, Ch32 L65; Book 2, Ch74 L14). A nondescript tattoo parlour run by Tilly is where Jason gets his crest (Book 2, Ch73 L62).

- **Ice cream**: Jason remembers kulfi-like ice cream from there (Book 7, Ch14 L52).

### 3. Not in the text

Jayapura's rulers, festival calendar and dress customs are not described in the lines read.

---

## Kurdansk: Culture of the People's Holy Federation of Dreisil

### 1. Summary

A river city in the People's Holy Federation of Dreisil, where celestines with dark skin and silver hair are common, entertainment and crime share the old docks, and prisons rather than indenture punish crime.

### 2. Facts

- **Nation**: Kurdansk lies "in the People's Holy Federation of Dreisil" (Book 6, Ch47 L390).
- **Celestines**: "Like many of Kurdansk's celestines, she had dark skin and silver hair", and the rowhouses are narrow and wedged together (Book 6, Ch47 L508).
- **Entertainment quarter**: a former warehouse district near the Kurdan River docks, now the most viable site for large theatre halls, "a heady mix of pleasure, criminality and money" (Book 6, Ch47 L436).
- **Skyport**: it has a busy skyport with towers where airships dock (Book 6, Ch47 L459).
- **The Reaper link**: Melody Jain's story begins in Kurdansk around twenty-five years before Book 9 (Book 9, Ch27 L24).

### 3. Using this in a story

Kurdansk is a crowded, mixed city of theatre halls, docks and celestine neighbourhoods; its prisons contrast with Estercost's indenture.

---

## Law and Indenture

### 1. Summary

### 2. Facts

- **Indenture, not slavery**: "We don't have slaves. We have indentured servants. A lot of criminals are sentenced to indenture, then their indenture is sold or auctioned to recoup the cost of their crimes" (Book 1, Ch92 L32). Jason finds the system disturbing and says both his world and this one need better systems (Book 1, Ch92 L33-34).
- **Captured enemies**: the Ustei do not accept slavery; "their culture doesn't allow warriors to be made slaves. For them, capture means release or death" (Book 1, Ch92 L36).
- **The Adventure Society agreement**: a clause in the Adventure Society's agreement with Greenstone gives anyone who completes a contract the right of refusal on anyone sentenced to indenture as a result of it (Book 1, Ch105 L66).
- **Hearings**: indenture hearings are a normal legal event (Book 2, Ch1 L77); the Greenstone court "was less like an American legal p[roceeding]" (Book 2, Ch3 L43).
- **Jason's argument**: if a family used "draconian measures" to hold people to cheap work, nothing would stop them (Book 1, Ch80 L78).

### 3. Using this in a story

Indenture is respectable in Estercost and a flashpoint with Earth-raised outsiders; scenes about it carry real moral weight.

---

## Leonids

### 1. Summary

Lion-headed, furred, strong and fast people; Gary is the best-known leonid.

### 2. Facts

- **Strength and stamina**: "Members of his leonid race are stronger and faster than us, and that's only the beginning of what leonids can do" (Book 1, Ch15 L50); a leonid needs far more exertion than others to tire, another racial gift (Book 1, Ch15 L84).
- **Appearance**: a huge "lion man" whose voice is growly by default (Book 1, Ch9 L131); Gary is "so huge he made bodybuilders look like they w[ere small]" (Book 1, Ch14 L14). He is covered in fur (Book 1, Ch15 L84).
- **Size**: draconians are larger even than male leonids (Book 2, Ch38 L29).
- **Mixed company**: a leonid, an elf and a celestine are among the eleven people who emerge from Dawn's cottage in Book 7 (Book 7, Ch52 L66).

### 3. Using this in a story

Leonids are natural front-liners: expect size, endurance and a deep voice.

---

## Merfolk

### 1. Summary

Merfolk are named once in the books as people, not fish; no detail beyond that is established here.

### 2. Facts

- **Only reference**: Emir answers a joke about someone's partner ("Man, woman or fish?") with "Merfolk are not fish. They happen to be very sensual people" (Book 3, Ch69 L509).
- **Open gap**: no chapter text found so far gives merfolk appearance, homeland or gifts.

### 3. Using this in a story

Use only the line above; any other merfolk detail is invention.

---

## The Mirror Kingdom: Culture of a Kingdom Ruled by a Diamond-Ranker

### 1. Summary

The Mirror Kingdom, also called the Kingdom of Mirrors, is a small, affluent kingdom founded and ruled for more than eight centuries by a diamond-ranker, the Mirror King. Its princes and princesses are many, and its prince Valdis leads an adventuring team in Greenstone.

### 2. Facts

- **Nature**: "the diminutive but influential Kingdom of Mirrors. Small, affluent and geographically blessed, it had neither expanded its borders nor been had its borders encroached upon in more than eight centuries" (Book 2, Ch51 L8).
- **The king**: the diamond-ranker known as the Mirror King founded the kingdom and rules it still; he has had "a series of queens, reportedly doting on each, even as they grew old and died beside him", and Valdis is one of the current queen consort's sons (Book 2, Ch51 L8).
- **Court manners**: kneeling is expected before the king; his protocol officer Hastor tells Jason "You should kneel", and the king says Hastor "starts getting snippy" without a seating chart by rank (Book 3, Ch5 L23, L34-36). Valdis's team drops to one knee on the king's arrival (Book 3, Ch5 L20). The king is amused, warm and teasing with his son (Book 3, Ch5 L40-48).
- **Teams built around princes**: Valdis's team was built around him, "the shaft to his spearhead" (Book 3, Ch3 L73-80). The kingdom has its own battles with the Builder cult (Book 3, Ch69 L895).
- **The king's aura**: Jason, an iron-ranker, finds the king's aura felt like part of the world, "in perfect symbiosis with the world", and it later inspires his own aura control (Book 4, Ch73 L98; Book 4, Ch74 L12).
- **Portals**: the king has his own portal specialist, Hastor, who calls up a glass-like portal (Book 3, Ch5 L50).

### 4. Not in the text

The kingdom's capital, size and food or dress customs are not given in the lines read.

---

## Outworlders

### 1. Summary

People whose souls re-entered a physical reality, manifesting a new body; they carry unusual gifts and cannot be easily tracked.

### 2. Facts

- **Definition**: Clive: "An outworlder is someone whose soul has re-entered a physical reality, reflexively manifesting a body for itself" (Book 2, Ch25 L57); the body is "akin to that of a monster, or a summoned familiar", physical substance forged from raw magic, between the astral and the physical (Book 2, Ch25 L59).
- **How it happens**: natural magical phenomena connecting two realities drag someone through; their body is annihilated in the astral and their soul reconstitutes one (Book 2, Ch25 L61).
- **Advantages**: Farrah says outworlders "might have their advantages", and Jason's abilities (map, interface, astral affinity) are outworlder abilities (Book 1, Ch15 L50, L74-76).
- **Children**: an outworlder with another species produces offspring of that species; two outworlders produce a celestine (Book 5, Ch44 L72).
- **Arrivals**: a hundred and nineteen outworlders arrive at once in Book 7 (Book 7, Ch36 L86); two outworlders among the eleven people in Dawn's cottage are bronze rank (Book 7, Ch52 L66).

### 3. Using this in a story

Outworlders are fish out of water: a body that is partly astral, gifts nobody can explain, and an arrival story that usually involves loss.

---

## Pranay: Culture of the Isolated City-States

### 1. Summary

The large southern land corresponding to Sri Lanka, where portal stopovers and isolated city-states form a trading hub, and where Jayapura is the port Hester calls home.

### 2. Facts

- **Geography**: Pranay is this world's equivalent of Sri Lanka, "larger and further south", a huge landmass in the Indian Ocean (Book 2, Ch34 L58). Its northern coast has several important sea-trade connections in a world without the Arabian Peninsula (Book 2, Ch69 L65).
- **Jayapura**: Hester's birth city; personal transport for those who can afford it is a platter-sized floating disc ridden by shifting weight (Book 2, Ch70 L3).
- **Mirage chambers**: Jayapura has a vast mirage chamber complex, more sophisticated than the Gellers' private chamber in Greenstone because the higher magic allows potent dimensional magic and concurrent chambers (Book 2, Ch73 L3).
- **Massive city**: Jason's team visited Pranay's urban centre after their first Reaper astral space and saw only familiars as magical beasts (Book 9, Ch33 L19).

### 3. Using this in a story

Pranay is the first place that shows Jason how rich a high-magic port can be; discs, mirage chambers and teleport stops are the texture.

---

## Religion in Practice

### 1. Summary

How faith appears day to day: crowded temple districts with hierarchies of their own, churches that serve as hospitals and archives, and gods whose rules limit what they can do.

### 2. Facts

- **Temple districts**: temples line the road to the Island's Divine Square and "gods had hierarchies of their own": the Temple of Roads sits behind the larger Temple of Journeys (Book 1, Ch45 L4). The square is crowded with booths selling religious paraphernalia and people proselytising (Book 1, Ch45 L5, L9).
- **Dominion**: "Dominion issues the divine right to kings and nobles. It is he who determines who rules, and who serves" (Book 1, Ch45 L18).
- **The Healer**: Neil makes a stand in the Temple of the Healer (Book 1, Ch94 L3); ex-clergy who offended their gods and were stripped of divinely gifted essences suffer a debilitating imbalance in body and soul (Book 4, Ch11 L16); the notes make the Healer the god who does this (notes, Book 1; the Healer is not named in that line).
- **Knowledge's temple**: in Rimaros it is "an important resource for the many magical researchers" with a symbiotic relationship with the Magic Society branch (Book 7, Ch9 L4); the temple is mostly a vast library covering multiple wings (Book 7, Ch9 L9).
- **Death's paperwork**: the Church of Death issues certification of how many times a person has died (Book 7, Ch17 L104).
- **The Hierophant**: the Hierophant of the World-Phoenix is allowed to intervene in Pallimustus a single time (Book 7, Ch60 L27).

### 3. Using this in a story

Scenes in a temple district should have noise, commerce and rival sects; clergy are practical people with real duties.

---

## Runics

### 1. Summary

Short, stocky, hairless people with deep-blue skin and naturally glowing runes.

### 2. Facts

- **Appearance**: "short and stocky, with skin of such a deep blue it was almost black", no hair and covered in faintly glowing, tattoo-like markings that are natural to the race (Book 1, Ch45 L14).
- **Rarity**: they are "a rarity in Greenstone" (Book 1, Ch45 L14); a runic barman in Old City is stocky and hairless with blue-black skin and glowing runes (Book 1, Ch71 L40).
- **Bones**: the runes show faintly on their bones; a short, broad skeleton in the desert astral space is identified as a runic by them (Book 1, Ch86 L31).
- **Notable**: Arash, a runic Jason meets in the temple district, explains Dominion's role: "Dominion issues the divine right to kings and nobles" (Book 1, Ch45 L14-18).

- **Part of the civilisation-building species**: the list of species that build civilisations is "humans, elves and leonids, celestines, runic, smoulder and draconians" (Book 1, Ch86 L40).
- **Common in Old City**: a runic works as a barman (Book 1, Ch71 L40), and a runic man appears at Jory's clinic (Book 1, Ch102 L72).

- **Inherent markings**: the glowing runes "looked like magic tattoos but were an inherent trait" (Book 7, Ch38 L10).
- **Spellcasters**: "Elves and runics were the most common affliction wielders, as their predilection for spells made a ranged power set much more likely" (Book 7, Ch15 L5).
- **Earth zones**: when zones turn Earth people into other species, Jason, Farrah and Dawn spot elves and "the dark-skinned runic people" (Book 5, Ch44 L56). A runic woman appears with Rick Mercer's team (Book 7, Ch59 L65).

### 3. Using this in a story

A runic in a scene stands out in Greenstone; their glowing marks make hiding difficult.

---

## Smoulder and Brighthearts

### 1. Summary

Dark-skinned people with glowing red eyes and affinities for earth and fire; the brighthearts are the smoulder of the deep underground realm of Cardinas.

### 2. Facts

- **Smoulder**: "They have natural affinities for earth and fire"; a smoulder friend of Humphrey's parents has the wind essence and his earth affinity evolved into an air affinity (Book 1, Ch85 L45). Niko, a member of Beth's team, has dark skin, glowing red eyes and jet-black hair (Book 1, Ch89 L63).

### 3. Using this in a story

Smoulder adventurers are ordinary Pallimustus people; brighthearts are a besieged, deep-living society that welcomes outsiders only after great loss.

---

## The Storm Kingdom: Culture of Rimaros and the Sea of Storms

### 1. Summary

A sea kingdom ruled from Rimaros, where a gold-rank monarch rules, heirs prove themselves as adventurers, and sky islands house the cream of society.

### 2. Facts

- **Extent**: "It rules the Sea of Storms, all the islands on it and all the coastal regions that surround it" (Book 7, Ch3 L47); the capital is Rimaros (Book 7, Ch3 L82).
- **Sky islands**: reserved for "the cream of society": big-name adventurers, long-standing adventuring families and aristocrats; a family known for producing good adventurers is mostly inducted into the nobility, and a house without good adventurers sinks into obscurity (Book 7, Ch5 L65).
- **Monarchy**: the Storm King is a gold-ranker, which one must be to take the throne; royals are not raised on cores as in most nations, since that disqualifies them here, and potential heirs from every branch are adventurers proving themselves in monster fighting and statecraft in a decades-long contest until the monarch chooses an heir (Book 7, Ch5 L67).
- **Titles**: the firstborn daughter of the Storm King, if competing, is the Hurricane Princess and the firstborn son the Storm Prince; many royals bow out, often adopting into their non-royal parent's family (Book 7, Ch5 L69).
- **Palindromic names**: first and last names that are palindromes are assigned in adulthood and mark important family members; the more prominent the family, the rarer the practice, and the royal family shares a name with the capital (Book 7, Ch18 L42).
- **Royal guard**: the Sapphire Crown is one of the most powerful guilds, backed by the royal family, and its non-royal members must periodically serve in the royal guard (Book 7, Ch21 L11).
- **Public islands**: Livaros, Arnote and Provo lie in an east-west line (Book 7, Ch7 L70). Arnote is mostly residential, home to successful merchants and retired adventurers, with a relaxed lifestyle that is part of Rimaros's cultural identity, and a minor royal branch lives there (Book 7, Ch7 L70, L72). Livaros is the "adventurer island", with the Adventure Society campus and most adventurer services (Book 7, Ch7 L78).

### 3. Using this in a story

Rimaros culture is about relaxed island life backed by sharp royal competition; do not assume a royal is soft because they smile.

---

## The Ustei: Northern Nomad Tribe

### 1. Summary

A nomad tribe from a region to the north that raids spirit coin convoys in the Greenstone desert and will not accept captured warriors as slaves.

### 2. Facts

- **Who they are**: the "sand pirates" are actually the Ustei Tribe, nomads from a region to the north (Book 1, Ch89 L50); Humphrey identifies them as northerners from their hair and clothes (Book 1, Ch81 L43).
- **The raids**: an expedition is formed to find out why they are so far south and stop them raiding spirit coin shipments (Book 1, Ch83 L4).
- **The response**: the best outcome is a peaceful return north or an agreement not to attack convoys; failing that, they are to be captured and interrogated (Book 1, Ch89 L50-51). Vincent expects them to talk if shown strength (Book 1, Ch89 L68).
- **Captives**: "Their culture doesn't allow warriors to be made slaves. For them, capture means release or death" (Book 1, Ch92 L36).

### 3. Using this in a story

The Ustei are proud, mobile raiders; treat them as a cultural unit rather than as bandits.

---

## The Ustei Homeland and the Ustei Way of Life

### 1. Summary

The Ustei are nomads from a region to the north of Greenstone, living on oases connected to an astral space and moving with their war barge. Ustei.md covers who they are; this file collects what the text says of their homeland and customs.

### 2. Facts

- **The homeland**: a region to the north of the Greenstone desert (Book 1, Ch89 L50), whose oases are connected to the astral space that supplies the desert (Book 1, Ch97 L21). When the astral space's water supply failed, the oases "were no longer able to support all the nomad tribes" (Book 1, Ch97 L21).
- **Travel**: the entire tribe lives on a war barge, "easily the equal of a passenger liner", built on giant bones with multiple decks (Book 1, Ch90 L14). See Desert_Transport_Skimmers_and_Barges.md.
- **Warriors and ranks**: only a fraction of the warriors are iron-rankers, most have one or two essences; bronze-rank captains lead them, and a silver-rank tribal chief commands the tribe (Book 1, Ch90 L8-9, L42).
- **Dress**: Humphrey identifies them as northerners "from the hair and clothes" (Book 1, Ch81 L43).
- **Women, children and slaves**: the tribe's women and children were locked away in conditions like slaves, and actual slaves were found in the same conditions (Book 1, Ch90 L44). Someone floated the idea of taking the Ustei men as slaves, "which would at least be something they understood" (Book 1, Ch92 L30).
- **Law of surrender**: "Their culture doesn't allow warriors to be made slaves. For them, capture means release or death" (Book 1, Ch92 L36). After defeat, they claim the barge and everything in it, including the women, children and slaves, belongs to the victor; "the idea is that now they have to go raiding for more women" (Book 1, Ch92 L12-16).
- **After the surrender**: the Adventure Society plans to relocate them north and investigate the astral space (Book 1, Ch97 L23).

- **Jason's regret**: in Book 4 he regrets his part in the battle and wonders whether a peaceful solution was tried (Book 4, Ch2 L29-31).

### 3. Not in the text

The homeland's name, geography beyond "north", the number of tribes and their religion are not given in the lines read.

---

## Valash

### 1. Summary

A skinny, chihuahua-headed species that is not native to Pallimustus; humans are turned into valash by Earth's transformation zones.

### 2. Facts

- **Origin**: Jason had only seen valash when Earth's transformation zones turned humans into them (Book 9, Ch45 L57); they are "a skinny sapient species not native to Pallimustus" (Book 9, Ch46 L5).
- **Appearance**: "skinny bodies and Chihuahua-like heads", shorter than Jason (Book 9, Ch45 L58; Book 9, Ch46 L5).
- **Outworlders**: the valash Jason meets in Yaresh is an outworlder with a silver-rank aura and no sign of core use (Book 9, Ch45 L58; Book 9, Ch46 L6).
- **Fashion**: his fitted white suit is not in the local style and "would have been more at home in a Miami nightclub in the eighties" (Book 9, Ch46 L5).

- **Zolit Kreen**: the valash outworlder introduces himself as "Zolit. Zolit Kreen" (Book 9, Ch46 L12). He was originally a valash, "attention-grabbing" for being an outworlder and a chihuahua-headed species, and "had spent years working very hard to undo that damage" (Book 9, Ch77 L4).
- **A niche**: he ranked to silver while staying as unremarkable as possible, then retired to be a fight promoter, where "everyone was a little bit strange" and "being a valash outworlder was his" gimmick (Book 9, Ch77 L4-5).
- **Prison**: Adventure Society enforcers lock him up after he meets another outworlder, asking about his assistant Benella and messengers (Book 9, Ch77 L6).
- **Social standing**: despite his small stature he is "not pushed, shoved, or disrespected" in a crowd, with a silver-rank aura (Book 9, Ch46 L6).

### 3. Using this in a story

Valash on Pallimustus are almost always outworlders from Earth's zones; they read as foreign and out of place.

---

## Vitesse: Culture of the City of Flowers

### 1. Summary

Vitesse is the garden-tower city of Estercost: wealthy, prosperous and full of powerful adventurers, with the Remore Academy and the continent's adventuring elite. This file covers how its people live; the geography is in `../06_Places/Vitesse.md`.

### 2. Facts

- **The city of flowers**: it is "known as the city of flowers", in what Jason would know as the French Riviera, with garden towers that have every third or fourth floor given to gardens "using water, light and plant magic to create lush refuges of peace" (Book 6, Ch47 L603). The towers house the wealthy elite, aristocrats and adventurers, and the headquarters of key organisations (Book 6, Ch47 L603).
- **Standing of the great families**: the Remore family has no aristocratic title and the Gellers only a small provincial one, and neither is begrudged residences in premier towers (Book 6, Ch47 L605).
- **Adventuring**: iron-rankers there may not hunt alone, because of the high magic (Book 2, Ch53 L27).

### 3. Using this in a story

Vitesse is a city where everyone is someone: expect good manners, good gardens and politics conducted through guild and academy membership.

### 4. Not in the text

Food, dress and festival customs of Vitesse are not described in the lines read.

---

## Yaresh: Culture of the Tree City

### 1. Summary

A small-population city that grows around its trees, whose civic government is weaker than its churches and societies, and whose two elf diamond-rankers dominate its adventuring scene.

### 2. Facts

- **Size and design**: only a few tens of thousands live there, but the design accommodates the environment so that the city has the footprint of a much larger centre (Book 9, Ch42 L26).
- **Walls and gates**: its walls are a line of massive trees with glossy black stone filling the gaps, tunnels pass through the trunks, and vehicles queue at a gate for inspection (Book 9, Ch43 L33).
- **Streets**: alleys are nicer than the norm and resemble gardens, with gravel paths through long grass (Book 9, Ch44 L3).

### 3. Using this in a story

Yaresh feels calm, green and old, but it is run by churches and societies, with landlords pulling strings.

---

## Zartos: Culture of a Celestine City Under the Earth

### 1. Summary

Zartos is a subterranean city built around an underground river, largely populated by celestines and home to the diamond-ranker Virid, Gary's mentor. The text shows it only as a place to work and rest.

### 2. Facts

- **Setting**: "a subterranean city built around an underground river, largely populated by celestines" (Book 7, Ch2 L75).
- **Celestine community**: Gary says "Zartos has a large celestine community" (Book 1, Ch16 L57); Rufus has not been, though his brother recommended it (Book 1, Ch16 L53-58). See Celestines.md for the people.
- **Craft**: Virid keeps a personal smithy there, "the best place to forge a great work" (Book 7, Ch2 L71-72). Gary and Virid forge while the team rests (Book 7, Ch2 L75).

### 3. Not in the text

Zartos's rulers, food, dress, festivals and religion are not described in the lines read.

---

## The Adventure Society

### 1. Summary

The Adventure Society is one of the three great organisations of Pallimustus. Its main purpose is monster extermination: it posts notices, issues contracts, assesses and registers adventurers, and keeps records of their kills. Its insignia is a **sword and rod crossed over a shield**, stitched on its staff's clothing and embossed on its badges (Book 1, Ch37 L93; Ch62 L61).

### 2. Joining

- **Application**: the applicant fills in personal details and undergoes a magical scan of their body and essences with an Adventure Society official, a Magic Society clerk (Russell, with a clipboard) and an acolyte of Knowledge, Gabrielle Pellin, who holds up a crystal orb as an aura reader; the essences she reads are written down (Book 1, Ch38 L118-149). Jason had already checked his combination against the **restricted list** (Book 1, Ch38 L135). Rufus tells Jason (Book 1, Ch20 L44) that it is impossible to become a member with a restricted essence; Death is restricted by itself and other restrictions are mostly combinations, because the confluence is usually the dangerous essence.
- **Field assessment**: an instructor takes a group of candidates out for a week, during which they must complete postings from the adventure boards of delta towns and villages (Book 1, Ch54 L35). Field testing takes place at the start of every month and candidates sign up at the administration desk (Book 1, Ch38 L158). Vincent Trenslow and Guy Spalding both act as instructors ("Instructor Trenslow" and "Instructor Spalding"; Book 1, Ch54 L36); Spalding is also Albert Bertinelli's supervisor. Adventurers also patrol outlying villages on a monthly rota, an unpopular punishment duty that means a month away from the city (Book 1, Ch25 L17; Ch64 L3).

### 3. Badges and stars

- **Badge**: a circular iron medallion embossed with the sword-and-rod-over-shield emblem, carried in a leather wallet (Book 1, Ch62 L61). It serves as identification, lets the holder copy a notice by touching the medallion to it and then to a blank sheet (Book 1, Ch32 L87), and is paired with a tracking stone: "It tells us if you're alive or dead, and lets us find you if you go missing or die" (Book 1, Ch62 L47). The Magic Society makes badges, taking an aura imprint in a magic circle (Book 1, Ch62 L46); Jason's tracking stone cannot track him because of his Mysterious Stranger gift (Book 1, Ch62 L47-54). Rufus's is bronze for his rank (Book 1, Ch32 L87).
- **Stars** show the Society's confidence in judgement, not power (Vincent, Book 1, Ch62 L81-85): two stars mean some unsupervised responsibility and different kinds of contract; three stars are "much the same as two, but more so". The notes add that stars drop when rank rises (Book 1, Ch62 L91); Lucian tells Pochard to put up the contract on an off-the-books spirit coin farm as a three-star contract so that "someone who'll actually do the work" takes it (Book 1, Ch42 L67), and Pochard answers that "if it involves the spirit coin farms, the Adventure Society will make it three-star" (Book 1, Ch42 L68); and Jason is later given a gold badge with a diamond symbol where one, two or three stars would normally be.
- **Stones for kills**: no awakening stones are awarded for killing monsters above the killer's rank (Vincent, Book 1, Ch91 L34).

### 4. Work

- **Notices** are posted on boards or issued directly; a notice carries "the name of the monster, or a description", the number of monsters and the approximate location (Book 1, Ch57 L33).
- **Contracts** are jobs: escort, extermination, investigation, delivery.
- **Orders and quotas** (Book 7, Ch12 L14-15): during a monster surge every adventurer has an **action quota** assigned at the jobs hall. A **contribution board** awards points for work beyond the quota, with weekly leaderboards and major rewards at the end of the surge. Guilds receive about **sixty percent** of the quotas; in a city like Livaros about three in ten adventurers are in a guild and one in ten in a high-end guild (Rufus, Book 7, Ch12 L23). Adventurers from guilds are served in a priority queue, local non-guild adventurers in a second, and outside adventurers in a third.
- **Builder-response team** (Book 7, Ch10 L124-129): an official refers Jason to this team for assessment as a potential threat before assigning a quota.

### 5. Structure and branches

- **Branch**: a director and deputy; in Greenstone the director is Elspeth Arella, the deputy Genevieve Picot (Book 1, Ch65 L60; Ch98 L63), and officials include Vincent Trenslow and Albert Bertinelli.
- **Continental council**: oversees the Society across a continent. In Book 2 an inquiry team from the council audits the Greenstone branch after the desert expedition disaster; its leader Tabitha Gert wears a black Adventure Society pin (Book 2, Ch10 L16). The Africa-like continent's council sits at Rakesh (Book 6, Ch47 L623).

### 6. Society and policy

- **Restricted essences**: Rufus says Death is restricted by itself (Book 1, Ch20 L44), and the Magic Society's living document shows one restricted combination, the Succubus essence, which takes two insanely rare essences (Book 1, Ch20 L104-110). A corrupt essence is on the list: a Magic-Society-bound adventurer hands one looted from an elemental to the Adventure Society "since it was on the restricted list" (Book 3, Ch7 L55); the Society offers good trades for restricted essences (Book 2, Ch65 L96). The wiki's fuller list and its Sacrifice-confluence pit fighter were not found in the text.
- **Neutrality**: the Society is described as neutral in disputes between nations (Book 8, Ch24 L22-25).

### 7. Hooks

- A newcomer's field assessment goes wrong.
- A continental inquiry arrives at a corrupt branch.
- A contribution-board dispute between a guild and independents.
- A restricted-essence user hides among adventurers.

### 8. Open questions

- (Resolved) The Society and the Magic Society do take part jointly in the application scan (Book 1, Ch38 L118-134).

---

## The Alchemy Association

### 1. Summary

The Alchemy Association is the guild of Pallimustus' alchemists. It lends its name to the potions and salves sold in a city, sits just behind the Adventure Society and the Magic Society in a city's guild district, and wants anything that advances alchemy, from Neil's miracle potion recipe to research in Yaresh. The text never describes its ranks or leadership.

### 2. Facts

- **Branding**: a tin of salve Jason buys in Book 1 is labelled "Greenstone Alchemy Association" (Book 1, Ch7 L25).
- **Membership**: Jory is "a member of the Adventure Society and the Alchemist Association" (Book 1, Ch36 L28). Belinda says the same of another person, "in the Alchemy Association and the Adventure Society" (Book 1, Ch76 L100). Dual membership is ordinary.
- **Standing**: in Greenstone's guild district the Forge Society and the Alchemist Association are "clearly second to the Adventure and Magic Societies, but still occupied impressive chunks of real estate" (Book 1, Ch38 L52).
- **Assistants**: Jason is told he could get assistants from the Association (Book 1, Ch61 L56).
- **Interest in recipes**: the Association has been "hounding" Neil for the miracle potion recipe (Book 3, Ch16 L80).
- **Benefits**: organisational benefits from the Adventure Society, Magic Society, Alchemy Association "and the like" are what the top guilds care about, not material rewards (Book 7, Ch12 L19).
- **Brokered supply**: a lord brokered the sale of a crate of potions through the Association, and stock runs short until people get their next batches from the vats (Book 7, Ch14 L74).

### 3. Not in the text

Its leadership, rank structure, dues, relation to the Artifice Association and what its name guarantees on a product are not stated in the lines read. The Artifice Association is mentioned only as a source of kitchen equipment and as a workshop where Travis works (Book 1, Ch91 L58; Book 7, Ch60 L94) and the Irios family are "deeply involved" in it in Rimaros (Book 7, Ch37 L62).

---

## Cults and Orders

### 1. Summary

### 2. The Order of the Reaper

See Order_and_Cult_of_the_Reaper.md for the full account of the order and the cult.

- **What it was** (Book 2, Ch25 L73-74; Book 3, Ch31 L25-27): an ancient cult of assassins who venerated the Reaper, "the finality of death". Emir says they were "scoured from this world, root and branch, by a coalition of churches" (Book 2, Ch25 L73). Shade explains (Book 2, Ch44 L99-109) that the churches, locked out of the sealed astral space, made a second bargain with the vorger, deep-astral entities that can violate dimensional boundaries; the vorger have no physical substance, appear in swarms and warp the bodies of those they affect until the city gains another flesh abomination. Shade says the Order served as royal assassins ("a knife in the dark can be the deliverance of nations"), was accepted by the nations, the Adventure Society, the Magic Society and most churches, and was also a hunter of the undead: "more necromancers fell to the Order than princes or kings"; the church of the Unliving and another church formed "an unholy compact" to remove it (Book 2, Ch44 L72-78).
- **The last grandmaster** (Book 2, Ch44 L85-89): when the churches found the training facilities beneath Sky Scar Lake, the last grandmaster sent the initiates into a sealed astral space (the City of Fallen Echoes) and scattered the keys, so that someone worthy might one day reclaim what was left. The grandmaster who built the place was an outworlder with the dark essence, "akin to Jason"; Shade was his summoned familiar.
- **Legacy**: the Way of the Reaper martial art and skill books; relics found by Emir. Shade tells Jason the Order "does not maintain a roster of assassins at all" but makes "connections. Quiet allies", matching jobs to people who think them worthwhile, a "volunteer network" whose tests find agents with the right principles (Book 2, Ch60 L120-123). The iron-only entry is from earlier notes and was not rechecked.

### 3. The Builder cult

- **Beliefs and methods**: the Builder recruits cults with gifts of power, uses **star seeds**, constructs such as golems and clockwork animals, and **Clockwork Kings**, whose cores convert essence users (Book 2, Ch25 L37-45; notes, Book 8: clockwork cores alter and take over bodies, so converts cannot use essence abilities because the cores cannot violate the soul; Book 8, Ch33 L18-19).

### 4. The Order of Redeeming Light

- **Effects and tools** (Book 8, Ch33 L18-19): the flames leave members with a distinct lack of imagination and a trend toward linear thinking. Pure servitors are essence users implanted with clockwork cores that the order has "purified" by putting a clockwork king through its rituals; the cores take over their bodies and block essence abilities. The order's principle is to take what is unclean, purify it and turn it into a weapon.
- **People** (Book 8, Ch18 L3-6): Melody Jain is the overall leader of the order's local operations, with her second Sendira and church adviser Laront (a Purity priest who never passed through the flames, so he can safely visit cities). Cell leaders are silver like their commanders, a source of constant tension; some resent Melody's use of the order for her own ends.

### 6. Hooks

- A reliquary of the Order surfaces in a collector's hands.
- A town goes quiet: its population have been "converted".
- A priest of Purity discovers the flames are a curse.
- A cult safe house with a clockwork king.

### 7. Open questions

- The Order of the Reaper's present state beyond the notes' account.
- The Builder cult's size and the Clockwork Kings' relationship to the Builder's world.

---

## Earth's Powers: the Network, the Cabal, the EOA and the Asano Clan

### 1. Summary

Earth has three old magical powers and, from Book 5 on, a fourth. The **Network** uses essence magic, the **Cabal** is older and tied to vampires and "old magic", and the **Engineers of Ascension** (EOA) is a radical group with a secret agenda. The **Asano clan** is Jason's family turned into a faction. Governments and the United Nations become players when magic is made public.

### 2. The Network

The full account is in `The_Network.md`; the notes below are the short form.

- **Ranks**: tiers or categories zero to five match the ranks on Pallimustus (Vermillion, Book 4, Ch11 L8, L19).
- **Going public** (Book 5, Ch17 L7): known to the public as the **Global Defense Network** (GDN) in the weeks after the monster waves began.
- **Methods**: tactical teams answer incursions with "hammer-blow tactics" that Jason tells Nigel will not hold against stronger monsters (Book 4, Ch41 L91-97). Lyon's branch concealed the Dimensional Fortress at Saint-Étienne for seventy years by faking the Grid (Book 4, Ch51 L30-37).

### 3. The Cabal

See The_Cabal.md for the full account.

### 4. The Engineers of Ascension (EOA)

See Engineers_of_Ascension.md for the full account.

- **Structure** (Book 5, Ch8 L14-18): four Cardinals: Mr North, Mrs South, Mr East and Mrs West. A "former Mrs South" gave the Network information about the necromancer (Book 6, Ch25 L98-99), so she left the EOA. Adrien Barbou becomes Mr East: "the previous Mr East" is "the late Mr East" (Book 5, Ch8 L28, L39), and Barbou is "now going by Mr East" beside Mr North (Book 5, Ch30 L43), "the new Mr East" with a new Mrs South (Book 5, Ch32 L5). In Book 6 Jason demands Barbou from Mr North, who refuses to give him up.
- **Reach**: took Perth, then Melbourne, then moved on Sydney (Book 4, Ch9 L76). Its League of Heroes appears at Broken Hill.

### 5. The Asano clan

See EDJI_and_Post_Reveal_Institutions.md for the clan as an organisation and the institutions built around it.

### 6. Governments and the UN

### 7. Hooks

- A Network team is outmatched; a stranger offers adventurer tactics.
- A Cabal fixer needs a favour from the family.
- An EOA cell approaches a talented young mage.
- A clan member leaves the domain against orders.

### 8. Open questions

- The Founder's identity and the details of the Grid's origin.
- The exact count and membership of the Network's fragments.

## The Engineers of Ascension

### 1. Summary

The Engineers of Ascension (EOA, also written EoA) is the youngest and weakest of Earth's three old powers: a union of outsider groups that tried to engineer a higher form of humanity and prepare for the day magic became public. Four "cardinals" ran it under Mr North, and its sabotage of the Grid ended the secrecy of magic and ended the organisation. Earth_Powers.md covers it briefly; this file is the full account.

### 2. Nature and aims

- **Outsiders joined together**: it is "largely made up of those who came to magic from outside the normal channels", formed by the strongest of the smaller groups "looking for strength in numbers", and its magic is "a hodgepodge, cobbled together from what they could beg, borrow, or steal" (Book 4, Ch11 L72).
- **Decentralised**: it operates in clusters, which makes it flexible but without cohesion, and "less likely to retaliate against the loss of individual members" (Book 4, Ch11 L64).
- **Engineering themselves**: it chooses its own path "into areas the hegemonic powers won't touch" and its name means they are trying to engineer themselves to a higher state (Book 4, Ch11 L83-84).
- **Preparing for the reveal**: its driving goal is to prepare for magic being revealed. It does not itself reveal magic, and plays along with secrecy while expecting the truth to come out (Book 4, Ch11 L86-88). It expects magic to become as valuable as money or political influence, and has been inducting influential private groups and individuals where the Network took the political ones (Book 4, Ch11 L97).
- **Products**: longevity treatments with few side effects, and more radical body modification (Book 4, Ch11 L99). Its "alchemically juiced-up thugs" are the foot soldiers (Book 4, Ch11 L144; Book 4, Ch12 L4).
- **Reach**: it seizes control of Perth and Melbourne and pushes into Sydney (Book 4, Ch9 L76). Hiro Asano's interests are bought out to get him out of its path (Book 4, Ch12 L41; Book 4, Ch13 L3).
- **Conflict with the Cabal**: its blood-servant research causes conflicts with Vermillion's organisation (Book 4, Ch11 L78). Compare The_Cabal.md.

### 3. The Four Cardinals

- **Adrien Barbou**: he starts as one of Mrs West's flunkies (Book 5, Ch3 L9) and proposes helping Asano rather than hammering him (Book 5, Ch3 L51). He becomes the new Mr East after his predecessor dies, and shows the previous Mr East had stalled the plan to give Cabal factions time to prepare (Book 5, Ch8 L26-28). Mr North poaches him from Mrs West's circle (Book 5, Ch32 L8).
- **The grid plan**: the EOA's final stage is to shut down the Grid. The self-repair function makes a wide-scale blackout difficult (Book 5, Ch3 L8-12). Barbou warns that the Grid will be down for months, not two weeks, which is an apocalypse (Book 5, Ch8 L44-47). Mr North decides to go ahead within a week (Book 5, Ch8 L54).
- **Mrs South's refusal**: the first Mrs South, Audrey, says the EOA's original goal was "to democratise magic", that they became what they fought, and that she will not burn the world down (Book 5, Ch8 L62). Mrs West tells her she will not leave the room, and she answers "I'll die clean" (Book 5, Ch8 L65-67). A new Mrs South is installed (Book 5, Ch32 L5).
- **Barbou's later conduct**: Gerling grabs him from the US (Book 6, Ch30 L29). He tells Gerling that he is "a vessel subject to the prevailing winds" and would change sides, and that Mr North knew the founder well (Book 6, Ch30 L58-60).
- **Network opponents**: Jack Gerling, a category-four Network essence user with the Onslaught essence, is sent to fight the vampires and later to fetch Barbou (Book 5, Ch47 L29-30; Book 6, Ch30 L29). Farrah Hurin, whom Barbou once held, is the key to the Network recovering the Grid (Book 5, Ch8 L50-51). See the Gerling and Farrah files.

### 4. After the sabotage

- **Splintering**: by the Nitra dome in Book 6 the EOA is splintering as the other factions hunt it, and it is blamed for the monster waves; one faction survives under a leader whose origins Jason knows are older than most of its members realise. Its strongest members are modified humans who dose themselves with magical drugs and pose as superheroes (Book 6, Ch1 L23, L28). They drop the superhero gimmick and operate more like a superhuman paramilitary (Book 6, Ch1 L29). Former members who did not know what they had joined are absorbed by the Global Defence Network (Book 6, Ch1 L27).

### 5. Later books (spoilers)

### 6. Using this in a story

Before the reveal the EOA is the aggressive newcomer with money, drugs and superheroes, and an internal fight over whether to burn the world. After the reveal its remnants are hunted or absorbed, and the damaged survivors of its experiments are a standing problem for the other factions.

## Guilds, Houses and Local Powers

### 1. Summary

A guild is an adventuring organisation that recruits, trains and backs its members and takes on a share of the Adventure Society's work. Guilds carry weight in the Society's surge quotas and in politics. Great families have their own guilds or schools; some local powers (the Aristocratic Faction) deliberately avoid personal power.

### 2. Burning Violet

Separate files in `Guilds/`: Burning_Violet.md, Sapphire_Crown.md, Guild_Quotas_and_Contribution.md and Team_Shining_Scabbard.md.

- **Relationship to the Academy**: after Rufus's grandfather became guild leader it became more associated with the Remore Academy (Book 6, Ch47 L125-129). Members are Rufus's family plus allies such as Gary and Farrah; most prestige-family graduates do not join, so it is mostly graduates without power or money. Farrah introduces herself in Rimaros as Burning Violet out of Vitesse (Book 7, Ch7 L53-54).

### 3. The Sapphire Crown

The royal family's guild in Rimaros (Book 7, Ch21 L11). The royal family support it and many royals are among its most capable members; in return every non-royal member must periodically serve in the royal guard. Membership is stringent, mostly from guild families; all members are examined quarterly, and while serving in the guard that rises to monthly or weekly. The royal family does not shield it from surge quotas: Trenchant Moore, a gold-ranker not currently serving in the guard, is responsible for a good portion of its quota. Power, not legacy, is the ultimate authority: Liara, a gold-ranker, commands more respect in the Crown and the Society than the silver-rank princess Vesper.

### 4. House Irios

Other houses have separate files in `Noble_Houses/`: Geller, Remore, Mercer, Rimaros, Headingway, Kettering, Davone and Amouz.

The pre-eminent producer of defensive engineering in the Storm Kingdom (Book 7, Ch18 L70): the defences of many cities and fortress towns, including Rimaros, are designed and maintained by the house. Its artificers take pride in the family, and Irios family members control Livaros's defences when it is attacked. Zara was matched with a member of the family (the cause of the scandal in Book 7, Ch18 L66-72). See `06_Places/Rimaros.md`.

### 5. Remore Academy

A school in Vitesse run by Roland Remore, the diamond arch-chancellor, who is rarely seen on campus but whose presence is felt (Book 3, Ch69 L587-591). Rufus, Farrah and others are graduates; connections only open the door to the prestigious guilds, and the Academy gives its graduates the skills to walk through it (Book 6, Ch47 L125-131).

### 6. The Aristocratic Faction of Yaresh (Level 2)

### 7. Hooks

- A newcomer's guild application is a political trap.
- A guild quota dispute during a surge.
- A royal guard rotation reveals a compromised member.
- The Aristocratic Faction tries to use a mirage arena as propaganda.

### 8. Open questions

- Other Vitesse guilds and their relative standing.
- Gilbert Bertinelli's tailor shop and the Bertinelli family's role in Vitesse.

## The Magic Society, the Associations and the MRA

### 1. Summary

### 2. The Magic Society

- **The living document** (Book 1, Ch20 L95-102): a marble-and-gold tablet, connected to a central record, that updates as the record changes and holds the full list of every essence and essence combination known to the Society. It is how Farrah and Rufus look up what a combination will produce.
- **Spirit coin farms**: registered farms exist (Clive, Book 1, Ch42 L58, L64: a spirit coin that did not come from "a registered spirit coin farm" implies an unregistered one that "needs to be found and shut down"). The Adventure Society treats anything touching the farms as three-star work.
- **Rakesh campus** (Book 6, Ch47 L623): the largest Magic Society and Adventure Society strongholds on the continent share a campus at Rakesh on the north coast. Portals work only in designated zones. Rakesh is divided into castes, and the campus holds Clive against his will, restricting his movements while using Belinda, whom it dismisses as unimportant, as a go-between. Clive realises the corruption he found in Greenstone is not unique.

### 3. The Alchemy and Artifice Associations

See Alchemy_Association.md, Mirage_Chamber_Association.md and Magical_Research_Association.md for the separate accounts.

Both are guilds. The Alchemy Association has been hounding Neil for the miracle potion recipe (Book 3, Ch16 L80); Jason jokes about complaining to the Artifice Association about shoddy standards (Book 3, Ch17 L60). Their internal structure is unchecked. In Greenstone's guild district the Forge Society and the Alchemist Association are "clearly second to the Adventure and Magic Societies, but still occupied impressive chunks of real estate" (Book 1, Ch38 L52).

### 5. Hooks

- A newcomer wants a combination looked up on the living document and finds it restricted.
- A scholar defects from the Magic Society to the MRA.
- A caste-bound researcher is held on a campus.
- The Alchemy Association wants a recipe.

### 6. Open questions

- The Magic Society's leadership above the branch level.
- The Alchemy and Artifice Associations' structure.
- Whether the Magic Society still has a "caste" system outside Rakesh.

## The Mirage Chamber Association

### 1. Summary

The Mirage Chamber Association is the body that owns and runs the mirage chamber complex in Jayapura, the biggest such facility the books show. A mirage chamber projects illusions that feel real to those inside; the Association turns that into a training service and a league sport, and it is powerful in its city for it. It is the only named body for mirage chambers; the Geller family's private chamber in Greenstone is not part of it.

### 2. What a mirage chamber is

- **The effect**: a chamber projects things from platforms along its walls (Book 1, Ch48 L7). Those inside feel everything as real, including the impact of blows, while their bodies lie unharmed in the control room (Book 1, Ch52 L5). Illusions can only affect, and be affected by, other illusions the chamber creates (Book 1, Ch48 L16).
- **Control**: a stone block under the control room window carries runes and diagrams; Rufus edits its monster imprints (Book 1, Ch48 L3, L13). Clive knows older stone-slab controls and has helped rebuild chambers in Boko and Hornis (Book 2, Ch17 L51, L60).
- **Limits**: low magical density restricts the Geller chamber to bronze-rank level for limited periods (Book 1, Ch37 L43). No chamber can simulate a whole world (Book 2, Ch20 L88).

### 3. The Jayapura complex

- **Scale**: the complex is larger and more sophisticated than the Gellers' private chamber. Higher magical density allows dimensional magic to replicate vast spaces and run several chambers at once (Book 2, Ch73 L3).
- **Business**: it hires out training space and is the city's premier entertainment venue, with essence users facing each other or illusory challenges before a paying audience (Book 2, Ch73 L4).
- **Standing**: the Association "had close ties with the Magic Society, Adventure Society and local government" and enjoys significant power and influence in Jayapura (Book 2, Ch73 L5).
- **Setting**: it is a flat circular building at the edge of the Mystic Quarter, spilling into the theatre district (Book 2, Ch73 L6).
- **Leagues**: two leagues a year in silver, bronze and iron divisions, leading to the grand championships; there is no gold division because the professionals stop using cores before gold (Book 2, Ch73 L10-12).
- **Management**: the place is run by former participants, now gold rank (Book 2, Ch73 L14).

### 4. Not in the text

The Association's membership, fees, rules beyond the leagues and presence in other cities are not stated. The Greenstone chamber belongs to the Geller family (Book 1, Ch37 L41-43).

## The Order and the Cult of the Reaper

### 1. Summary

Two different bodies carry the Reaper's name. The **Cult of the Reaper** is the old body of worshippers of the great astral being called the Reaper, who venerate "the finality of death" and work alongside the Church of Death. The **Order of the Reaper** is a faction that split from the cult to become assassins and political power-brokers, was "destroyed" by a coalition of churches, and has quietly returned. Cults_and_Orders.md holds the short entries; this file is the full account.

### 2. The old Order

- **What it was**: an ancient cult of assassins who venerated the Reaper. Emir says they were "scoured from this world, root and branch, by a coalition of churches" (Book 2, Ch25 L73).
- **Its place**: Shade says the Order served as royal assassins ("a knife in the dark can be the deliverance of nations"), was accepted by the nations, the Adventure Society, the Magic Society and most churches, and hunted the undead: "more necromancers fell to the Order than princes or kings". The church of the Unliving and another church formed "an unholy compact" to remove it (Book 2, Ch44 L72-78).
- **The last grandmaster**: when the churches found the training facilities beneath Sky Scar Lake, the last grandmaster sent the initiates into a sealed astral space and scattered the keys, so that someone worthy might one day reclaim what was left (Book 2, Ch44 L85-89). The churches, locked out, made a second bargain with the vorger, deep-astral entities that warp those they touch into flesh abominations (Book 2, Ch44 L99-109).
- **Legacy**: the Way of the Reaper martial art and its skill books, and relics found by Emir. Shade tells Jason the Order "does not maintain a roster of assassins at all" but a "volunteer network" of quiet allies (Book 2, Ch60 L120-123).

### 3. The cult and the split

- **Skill books**: the Order's techniques are designed from the foundation to include skill-book use (Book 4, Ch36 L8).
- **The cult**: it "operates in that world, or it once did", but the Reaper does not speak to it directly, which would trespass on the local god of death (Book 4, Ch3 L30). Callum Morse says the cult venerates "the principles of the Reaper. The sanctity of death" and has long worked alongside the Church of Death. The Church does public-facing things and the cult can be its "hidden dagger" (Book 6, Ch47 L588-590; Book 9, Ch27 L87).
- **Cult versus order**: "The Order of the Reaper is an offshoot of the cult", a faction that wanted to become more active in the world and accrue political power (Book 6, Ch47 L590). The order "split from the cult, mouthing affinity to the Reaper while abandoning the principles", became self-serving assassins and then falsified its demise. The Sky Scar Lake remnant was a faction that wanted reconciliation with the cult and was "sacrificed for it" (Book 6, Ch47 L592).
- **Membership**: veneration must be authentic. The cult is hard to infiltrate because it is impossible to fake long-term (Book 9, Ch27 L57). Arabelle summarises the difference: the cult follows the Reaper's principles; the order is the offshoot (Book 9, Ch27 L22-23).
- **Shade's contact**: before returning to Earth, the World-Phoenix's avatar contacted the cult's counterpart and persuaded him to let Shade, a shadow of the Reaper, act as a vessel (Book 5, Ch56 L69).

### 4. The order's return (Book 9)

Arabelle tells Jason the story as Callum gave it to her (Book 9, Ch27).
- **The long plan**: the order is bringing "centuries of planning" to fruition around twenty-five years before (Book 9, Ch27 L24). It faked the demise of its entire order as part of a plan "more than half a millennium in the making" (Book 9, Ch27 L48).
- **Method**: it infiltrates other organisations, including low-level administrative staff of churches, where deep faith is not required (Book 9, Ch27 L26). Its aim is to return to being an open secret "playing tool to those in power while pulling the levers of power themselves"; the first time, it was "too crude and got crushed" (Book 9, Ch27 L50).
- **Strikes against threats**: it made critical strikes against the Church of Purity, the Cult of the Builder and other threats, having infiltrated them and let them grow dangerous first (Book 9, Ch27 L50-51). The cult suspects it also knew about the messengers (Book 9, Ch27 L54).
- **Melody Jain**: a highly capable member from "one of the old order families". She hid a husband and daughter, Sophie, and was discovered when Sophie was small (Book 9, Ch27 L24-26).
- **Marta Fries**: Melody's best friend in the order, who warned her and helped smuggle the husband and Sophie out (Book 9, Ch27 L30). Fries is under scrutiny after Melody's defection, and later helps trace Melody (Book 9, Ch27 L91).
- **Callum Morse**: a cult member, made Melody's handler when she came to the cult. They investigated the order together for years, but the cult never truly accepted her (Book 9, Ch27 L47, L63). He is in love with her (Book 9, Ch27 L65, L78).
- **The search**: Callum steered Emir and a diamond-rank historian friendly to the cult towards collecting Way of the Reaper skill books, hoping a stray user of the techniques would turn up Melody's husband (Book 9, Ch27 L65-73). That is how Sophie and Emir came to Marta Fries (Book 9, Ch27 L81).
- **The Purity infiltration**: the cult required Melody to infiltrate the Order of Redeeming Light as a momentous gesture before it would accept her, over Callum's objection (Book 9, Ch27 L83-85). She was subjected to the "cleansing fire" and became an artificial zealot, and Callum lost track of her (Book 9, Ch27 L90-91).

### 5. Later books (spoilers)

### 6. Using this in a story

Use the cult for stern, principled men and women who will not betray the dead, and the order for patient infiltrators who may be anywhere. Someone claiming to follow "the Reaper's way" is probably cult; someone offering a favour and a polite knife is probably order. Remember that the order can be inside any church's administration.
